Unified High-Probability Analysis of Stochastic Variance-Reduced Estimation


Abstract
Stochastic estimators are fundamental to large-scale optimization, where population quantities must be inferred from noisy oracle observations. Although influential methods such as momentum, SPIDER, STORM, and PAGE have been highly successful, their analyses are largely estimator-specific and expectation-based, obscuring the structural tradeoffs that determine reliability. In this paper, we develop a unified framework for stochastic variance-reduced estimation based on a recursion with three components: memory retention, reset probability, and a correction term for iterate movement. This framework recovers several classical estimators, motivates new second-order variants, and yields a bias-variance decomposition of estimation error. Our main result is a unified high-probability bound proved using a new dimension-free vector-valued Freedman inequality, valid for smooth normed spaces involving random sums of vector martingales. The result applies in both Euclidean and non-Euclidean settings, including the analysis of mirror-descent-based methods in Banach spaces. As applications, we obtain high-probability oracle complexities for unconstrained optimization with mirror descent, establishing the logarithmic dependence on the confidence level. We also derive the first 𝒪 ~ ​ ( ε − 3 ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-3}) oracle-complexity bounds for stochastic optimization with expectation constraints, improving upon the existing 𝒪 ~ ​ ( ε − 4 ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-4}) complexity by leveraging variance-reduced estimation for the first time in this setting.

1  Introduction

Stochastic optimization algorithms optimize a function over iterations by using noisy oracle information rather than exact population quantities. In the most familiar setting
[
118
,
113
,
106
,
47
]
, the quantity of interest is the population gradient, and standard first-order algorithms must repeatedly construct accurate gradient estimates from stochastic samples to make progress. From this perspective, the effectiveness of a first-order method depends not only on how it updates the iterate, but also on how well it maintains an estimator that is accurate, stable, and inexpensive to compute as the iterate evolves. Many widely used methods can be viewed in this way. Classical momentum stabilizes SGD by averaging past gradients
[
91
]
, whereas variance-reduced methods, such as SVRG, STORM, and PAGE, reuse information across iterates through stochastic differences and occasional reset steps
[
77
,
43
,
33
,
86
,
132
]
.

Although these methods are often presented as distinct algorithmic ideas, they address a common underlying problem: how to recursively estimate an evolving population quantity from noisy samples while controlling both bias and variance. Despite the empirical successes of these methods, however, the theoretical picture remains fragmented. Existing analyses are typically developed separately for individual estimators, and their proofs are often tightly tied to the algebra of a particular recursion. As a result, it is difficult to isolate which design choices are actually responsible for performance: the reuse of past information, the frequency of resets, or the way past information is adjusted as the iterate moves. In addition, most existing guarantees are expectation-based. While such guarantees characterize average behavior, they can conceal rare but important estimation failures, which are especially relevant in large-scale learning and in constrained optimization.

In this paper, we develop a
unified framework for high-probability analysis of stochastic variance-reduced estimation in optimization
. We consider a general population quantity
g(w_{t})
observed through an unbiased stochastic oracle
G(w_{t},\xi_{t})
, where the iterate
w_{t}
changes over time
t
, and
\xi_{t}
denotes the oracle randomness. We introduce an estimator for
g(w_{t})
governed by
three simple design choices:
1) how much past information must be retained, 2) how often resets using fresh estimates occur, and 3) how past information computed at
w_{t-1}
must be corrected so that it remains informative at the current iterate
w_{t}
. This viewpoint places several seemingly different recursive estimators within a single template and makes it possible to compare them through a common error decomposition. Beyond gradient estimation, the framework also applies to other optimization-relevant quantities.

\hskip-25.60747pt\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}f(w)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[F(w,\xi)]\vphantom{\bigg|}

(
\dagger
)

\hskip-8.53581pt\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[F(w,\xi)]\;\text{ s.t. }\;h(w)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[H(w,\xi)]\leq 0.\vphantom{\bigg|}

(
\ddagger
)

The two representative examples above illustrate the scope of our framework. In problem (

†

‣
1
), the relevant quantity is the population gradient
g(w)=\nabla f(w)
, and the goal is to maintain an accurate stochastic estimate of the gradient along the optimization trajectory. In problem (

‡

‣
1
), however, the quantity of interest is the constraint value
h(w)
, which must be estimated from noisy samples to assess feasibility
[
83
]
. In this setting, poor estimation can lead either to unreliable feasibility detection or to overly conservative large-batch procedures. This paper, therefore, studies a broader estimation problem in stochastic optimization, with stochastic gradient estimation and expectation-constraint estimation as two central instances.

Another limitation of the existing literature is that high-probability guarantees are predominantly restricted to Euclidean geometries. In more general first-order methods, such as mirror descent
[
14
,
12
,
22
]
, the estimator naturally lives in the dual space rather than the primal space. The literature currently lacks a unified, high-probability concentration framework that accommodates arbitrary smooth Banach spaces without relying on estimator-specific algebra. Our analysis in this paper is developed at a level of generality that covers both standard Euclidean updates and generalized first-order methods such as mirror descent, while focusing on high-probability guarantees.
Please see Appendix
G
for a detailed discussion of related work.

Contributions.
Our main contributions are as follows:

•

Unified estimator framework.
We propose a single recursive stochastic estimator (

∗

‣
3
) that subsumes classical momentum, STORM, PAGE, and SPIDER as special cases, while also suggesting new second-order variants; see Table
1
.

•

Unified high-probability analysis.
We prove a general high-probability bound for the estimation error of every estimator in the framework, together with an explicit bias-variance decomposition; see Theorem
4.2
. The proof relies on a new vector-valued Freedman inequality; see Theorem
B.2
.

•

Unconstrained optimization.
We establish high-probability guarantees for finding first-order
\varepsilon
-stationary points in stochastic optimization with mirror descent, and we derive the resulting oracle complexities for different estimator choices; see Table
5
and Theorem
E.1
in Appendix
E

•

Constrained optimization.
We provide the first variance-reduced oracle complexity guarantees for expectation-constrained stochastic optimization, improving the rate from
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-4})
in
[
83
]
to
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-3})
; see Table
3
.

2  Preliminaries

We briefly introduce the notations and setup used throughout the paper. Let
(\mathcal{X},\|\cdot\|)
be a reflexive Banach space, and let
(\mathcal{X}^{*},\|\cdot\|_{*})
denote its dual space of all continuous bounded linear functionals. For
u\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we write
\langle u,x\rangle
for the canonical duality pairing between
\mathcal{X}^{*}
and
\mathcal{X}
such that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. The dual norm is defined by
\|u\|_{*}\coloneqq\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1,x\in\mathcal{X}}\langle u,x\rangle
for any
u\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
. The optimization iterates lie in a nonempty closed, convex set
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
.

2.1
Mirror Map.

The mirror map
\Phi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
with
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
is proper, weakly lower semi-continuous (w.l.s.c), and
1
-strongly convex on a nonempty closed convex set
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\text{int}(\mathcal{D})
with respect to
\|\cdot\|
. Furthermore,
\Phi
is Gâteaux differentiable on
\mathcal{W}
.

2.2
Proximal Gradient Mapping.

For
w\in\mathcal{W}
, an input vector
u\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, and a step size
\eta>0
, the
proximal gradient mapping
is

P(w,u,\eta)=(w-w^{+})/\eta
,
where the updated point
w^{+}
is the unique minimizer of the proximal subproblem:

w^{+}=\arg\min_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}}\langle\eta u,w^{\prime}\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)

and
D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)
denotes the Bregman divergence induced by
\Phi
.

The existence and uniqueness are guaranteed by variational principles. The strong convexity of
\Phi
renders the objective strictly convex and coercive, while its closed convexity ensures it is w.l.s.c.
[
42
, Cor. 2.2, p. 11]
. By the Mazur’s Lemma and Eberlein-Šmulian theorem
[
21
, Thm. 3.7, 3.18]
, coercivity within the reflexive space
\mathcal{X}
forces bounded sequences to converge weakly to a feasible limit inside the closed set
\mathcal{W}
. Consequently, the objective reliably attains a unique global minimum
[
42
, Prop. 1.2, p. 35]
,
[
11
, Thm. 2.11, p. 72]
.
Based on variational inequality as supported by
[
42
]
, we derive properties of
P(w,u,\eta)
(see Lemma
A.6
and
[
46
,
12
]
), including
\|P(w,u,\eta)\|_{*}\leq\|u\|
.

We consider a stochastic oracle
G:\mathcal{W}\times\Omega\to\mathcal{G}
and its population counterpart
g(w)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[G(w,\xi)],
where
\xi
denotes the oracle randomness,
\mathcal{G}
is a
\kappa
-smooth (
\kappa\geq 1
) normed space with its norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}}
. For high-probability analysis, we introduce
C(\delta,\kappa)\coloneqq\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{3\ln(1/\delta)}
for
\delta\in(0,1)
, arising from our proposed vector-valued Freedman inequality (see Theorem
B.2
).
Our goal is to estimate the time-varying target
g(w_{t})
along a trajectory
\{w_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Given a recursive estimator
v_{t}
, we define the estimation error
e_{t}\coloneqq v_{t}-g(w_{t}).
Our analysis is not restricted to Euclidean SGD and applies more broadly to generalized first-order methods, including mirror descent
[
14
,
12
,
22
]
.

Mirror Descent Update.
At each iteration
t
, we form a unified estimator
v_{t}
and use its corresponding update vector
U_{t}
, to perform a mirror descent update with step size
\eta_{t}>0
:

w_{t+1}=\arg\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\left\{\eta_{t}\langle U_{t},w\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w,w_{t})\right\}=w_{t}-\eta_{t}P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t}).

(1)

2.3
Boundedness of Generalized Updates.

Let
U_{t}
be the update vector computed at iteration
t
. We assume the algorithm employs a normalization or clipping mechanism such that the update vector is bounded almost surely, satisfying
\|U_{t}\|_{\ast}\leq G
for all
t\geq 0
.

We note that this boundedness assumption, while necessary, is not as strong since in many problems it can be ensured by applying normalization or clipping operations (e.g., Application 1 in Subsection
6.1
), or holds due to certain Lipschitzness conditions (e.g., Application 2 in Subsection
6.2
). Under the above assumption, we show that
\|w_{t+1}-w_{t}\|=\eta_{t}\|P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})\|\leq\eta_{t}\|U_{t}\|_{*}\leq\eta_{t}G
.

3  A Unified Estimator

In stochastic optimization, estimating an evolving population quantity
g(w_{t})
from noisy observations
G(w_{t},\xi_{t})
requires combining fresh information with historical memory. A classical example is momentum
[
91
]
that filters noise via
v_{t}=(1-\beta)G(w_{t},\xi_{t})+\beta v_{t-1}
. If we define the estimation error by
e_{t}\coloneqq v_{t}-g(w_{t})
, then the corresponding error recursion by
e_{t}=\beta e_{t-1}+(1-\beta)(G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-g(w_{t}))-\beta(g(w_{t})-g(w_{t-1}))
. The final term captures the deterministic lag induced by reusing information from the previous iterate.
Variance-reduced estimators, such as STORM and SPIDER, avoid this deterministic bias by incorporating stochastic differences. This suggests a common design principle for recursive estimators: once past information is carried over from iteration
t-1
, it should be corrected before being reused at the new iterate
w_{t}
. We make this principle explicit through an explicit
correction term

\mathcal{T}_{t}
, which leads to a unified recursion governed by three design choices: memory retention
\beta_{t}
, the reset probability
p_{t}
, and the correction mechanism
\mathcal{T}_{t}
.

The Unified Recursion.

Let
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the filtration generated by oracle noise and reset events (see Appendix
A
). We assume parameter predictability and sub-Gaussian oracle noise stated below.

3.1
Parameter Predictability.

The parameters
\eta_{t-1}
,
p_{t}\in[0,1]
, and
\beta_{t}\in[0,1]
are
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-adapted. The reset indicator
b_{t}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p_{t})
is sampled at time
t
, ensuring
\mathbb{E}[b_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=p_{t}
.

3.2
Sub-Gaussianity.

The oracle is unbiased,
\mathbb{E}[G(w_{t},\xi)\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=g(w_{t})
. Furthermore, for any fresh sample
\xi
drawn at time
t
(whether a single sample
\xi_{t}
or a component
\xi_{t}^{(i)}
of a batch of
B
independent samples
\xi_{t}^{B}\equiv(\xi_{t}^{(i)})_{i=1}^{B}
), the noise is conditionally sub-Gaussian with variance proxy
\sigma^{2}>0
, satisfying
\mathbb{E}[\exp(\|G(w_{t},\xi)-g(w_{t})\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}/\sigma^{2})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq 2
.

At iteration
t
, given the reset indicator
b_{t}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p_{t})
, we define the unified estimator as:

v_{t}\coloneqq\begin{cases}G(w_{t},\xi_{t}^{B}),&b_{t}=1,\\
G(w_{t},\xi_{t})+\beta_{t}\Delta_{t}+\mathcal{T}_{t}(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t}),&b_{t}=0,\end{cases}

(
\ast
)

where
\xi_{t}^{B}
represents a fresh batch of samples,
\xi_{t}
is a single stochastic sample, and
\Delta_{t}\coloneqq v_{t-1}-G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t})
. Viewed through this lens,
\mathcal{T}_{t}
is the mechanism that corrects stale information from the previous iterate. Different choices of
\mathcal{T}_{t}
recover classical estimators and naturally suggest new ones.

3.1 Error Dynamics and Effective Innovation

We next derive the common error recursion induced by (

∗

‣
3
). When a reset occurs, i.e.,
b_{t}=1
, the estimator is reinitialized using a fresh stochastic estimate. The more interesting case is the recursive steps
b_{t}=0
, where past estimation error is propagated and modified by the correction mechanism.

Expanding
e_{t}=v_{t}-g(w_{t})
and substituting
v_{t-1}=e_{t-1}+g(w_{t-1})
yields the core error recursion
e_{t}=\beta_{t}e_{t-1}+\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})
. The estimation error thus consists of a contracted past error and a stochastic source term, which we define as the
Effective Innovation
.

3.3
Effective Innovation.

The effective innovation
\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})
at iteration
t
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\coloneqq(1-\beta_{t})\underbrace{(G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-g(w_{t}))}_{\text{Direct Noise }}+\beta_{t}\underbrace{\Delta(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t})}_{\text{Difference Noise}}+\mathcal{T}_{t}(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t}),

(2)

where
\Delta(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t})\coloneqq(G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t}))-(g(w_{t})-g(w_{t-1}))
is the centered difference.

The decomposition in (
2
) makes the bias-variance structure of the unified recursion. The conditional expectation of
\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})
determines the predictable bias, while its variability drives the variance.

Specific choices of the correction term
\mathcal{T}_{t}
.

By specifying
\mathcal{T}_{t}
, we recover existing variance-reduced methods and suggest new higher-order variants, summarized in Table
1
.

•
Family 1: Zeroth-Order Correction (Standard Recursion and Resetting).

Setting

\mathcal{T}_{t}\coloneqq\beta_{t}\bigl(G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t})-G(w_{t},\xi_{t})\bigr)
in (

∗

‣
3
)
recovers the momentum-style update

v_{t}=(1-\beta_{t})G(w_{t},\xi_{t})+\beta_{t}v_{t-1}.

This attenuates direct oracle noise through averaging, but introduces a deterministic bias induced by iterate movement, since information formed at
w_{t-1}
is reused at
w_{t}
.

•
Family 2: First-Order Correction (Differential Recursion and Resetting).

By setting
\mathcal{T}_{t}\coloneqq 0
, the recursion in (

∗

‣
3
) is driven entirely by stochastic differences. This eliminates the deterministic drift inherent to momentum updates, leaving an estimation error governed solely by centered difference noise that scales with
\|w_{t}-w_{t-1}\|
. This formulation recovers STORM (
\beta_{t}<1
), PAGE (
p_{t}>0,\beta_{t}=1
), and SPIDER (periodic resets).

•
Family 3: Second-Order Correction (Second-Order Recursion and Resetting).
Letting
\mathcal{T}_{t}
encode a Taylor correction,
\mathcal{T}_{t}\coloneqq\beta_{t}\bigl(G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t})-[G(w_{t},\xi_{t})+\nabla G(w_{t},\xi_{t})(w_{t-1}-w_{t})]\bigr),
incorporates local curvature information in (

∗

‣
3
). This reduces the leading-order deterministic bias to a second-order residual, while the remaining stochastic error is governed by the Hessian-vector product.

Table 1:
Special cases of the unified estimator. Here,
\Delta G:=\beta_{t}(G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t})-G_{t}(w_{t},\xi_{t}))
,
2^{\text{nd}}\coloneqq\beta_{t}\bigl(G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t})-[G(w_{t},\xi_{t})+\nabla G(w_{t},\xi_{t})(w_{t-1}-w_{t})]\bigr)
, and
\mathbb{I}_{\mathsf{Sched}}
refers to a deterministic schedule (e.g.,
t\mod E=0
). Bolded entries are
new
estimators.
SO
denotes Second-Order.

4  Unified Estimation Error Bound

We now develop a high-probability bound for the unified estimator introduced in Section
3
. The error recursion derived in Section
3.1
shows that the estimation error is driven by the accumulation of effective innovations over epochs delimited by reset times. Our goal in this section is to control this accumulation uniformly with high probability.
We first impose a regularity condition on the effective innovation, separating bias from the centered stochasticity and enabling martingale concentration.

4.1
Conditional Regularity of Innovation.

We assume there exist non-negative, predictable processes
\{\mathcal{B}_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
and
\{\Sigma_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
adapted to
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
such that, almost surely

i)
The conditional bias is bounded by
\mathcal{B}_{t}
, i.e.,
\|\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\mathcal{B}_{t}.

ii)
The centered innovation
\mathcal{Z}_{t}\coloneqq\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
is sub-Gaussian conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
with a random, predictable variance proxy
\Sigma_{t}^{2}
. That is,
\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\|\mathcal{Z}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}}{\Sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\leq 2.
We also assume that on the
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable event
\{\Sigma_{t}=0\}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{t}=0
almost surely.

Assumption
4.1
controls the effective innovation by bounding its conditional bias
\mathcal{B}_{t}
and its centered stochastic component via the variance proxy
\Sigma_{t}^{2}
. We now state the main theorem for estimation error.

4.2
Unified Estimation Error Bound.

Consider the unified estimator (

∗

‣
3
) governed by predictable sequences
\{\beta_{t}\}_{t\geq 1},\{p_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
and
\{\eta_{t-1}\}_{t\geq 1}
satisfying Assumption
3.1
. Assume
b_{0}=1
and, without loss of generality,
b_{T}=1
for some deterministic
T
. Let
\tau_{m}
denote the reset times, defined recursively by
\tau_{0}=0,

\tau_{m}=\inf\{t>\tau_{m-1}:b_{t}=1\}
for
1\leq m\leq T,
and define the cumulative momentum factors
\Lambda_{t,j}\coloneqq\prod_{i=j+1}^{t}\beta_{i},

A_{t}\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{t}\beta_{i}^{-1}
, with the convention
\Lambda_{t,t}=1
. Let
m(t)
denote the unique index such that
t\in(\tau_{m(t)-1},\tau_{m(t)}].
Under Assumptions
3.2
,
4.1
, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
and any deterministic variance budget
V_{t}\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the condition
\mathfrak{V}_{t}^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{j=\tau_{m(t)-1}+1}^{t}A_{j}^{2}\Sigma_{j}^{2}\leq V_{t}
implies that the estimation error satisfies

\|e_{t}\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C\left(\frac{\delta}{2T},\kappa\right)\Lambda_{t,\tau_{m(t)-1}}\sigma/\sqrt{B}+\sum_{j=\tau_{m(t)-1}+1}^{t}\Lambda_{t,j}\mathcal{B}_{j}+C\left(\frac{\delta}{2T},\kappa\right)A_{t}^{-1}\sqrt{V_{t}}.

(3)

The bound in Theorem
4.2
decomposes the estimation error into three terms. The first term captures the effect of the most recent reset or batch initialization and decays according to the cumulative momentum since that reset. The second term accumulates the conditional bias of the effective innovations through the quantities
\mathcal{B}_{j}
. The third term is a high-probability concentration term controlled by the predictable variance accumulated over the current reset interval. This decomposition makes explicit how reset probability, momentum strength, and the choice of correction term jointly determine the quality of the estimator. The complete details of the proof are presented in Appendix
B
.
The technical difficulty in proving this result requires establishing concentration bounds for sums of martingale difference sequences over random reset times.
To handle this, we combine a masking argument for stopping-time intervals (see Lemma
B.1
) with the vector-valued Freedman inequality (see Theorem
B.2
), which is the main concentration tool underlying the proof.

5  Instantiations of the Unified Estimation Error Bound

Theorem
4.2
reduces the analysis of any estimator in our unified framework to controlling two quantities associated with the
Effective Innovation
: the conditional bias bound
\mathcal{B}_{t}
and the predictable variance proxy
\Sigma_{t}^{2}
in Assumption
4.1
. In this section, we instantiate these quantities for the three families. The resulting bounds reveal how
\mathcal{T}_{t}
dictates the bias-variance tradeoff.
Our standing assumptions from previous sections remain in play throughout. In particular, we use Assumption
2.3
to control the displacement of consecutive iterates, Assumption
3.1
for the predictability of the control sequences, and Assumption
3.2
for the sub-Gaussianity of the oracle.
The only additional ingredients needed in this section are family-specific regularity conditions that allow us to identify
\mathcal{B}_{t}
and
\Sigma_{t}^{2}
in Assumption
4.1
concretely.

Beyond Assumptions
2.3
,
3.1
, and
3.2
, we require the additional assumptions associated with the three estimator families. These enable us to satisfy Assumption
4.1
and apply Theorem
4.2
to bound the estimation error
\|e_{t}\|_{\mathcal{G}}
. All proofs are available in Appendix
C
.

5.1 Family 1: Zeroth-Order Correction

We begin with the zeroth-order family, which recovers the momentum-style update, where the correction term is

\mathcal{T}_{t}(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t})=\beta_{t}\bigl(G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t})-G(w_{t},\xi_{t})\bigr)
, and its effective innovation is
\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})=(1-\beta_{t})(G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-g(w_{t}))-\beta_{t}(g(w_{t})-g(w_{t-1}))
.

5.1
Lipschitz Continuity.

The mean function
g
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous, satisfying
\|g(w)-g(w^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq L\|w-w^{\prime}\|
for all
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
.

Assumption
5.1
controls the drift caused by reusing past information at
w_{t-1}
while the target has moved to
g(w_{t})
.
Under Assumption
5.1
, we show that the conditional regularity of the innovation (Assumption
4.1
) is satisfied. This holds because the conditional bias
\mathcal{B}_{t}
is bounded as follows:

\|\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\|_{\mathcal{G}}=\beta_{t}\|\mathbb{E}[g(w_{t})-g(w_{t-1})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\beta_{t}\eta_{t-1}LG=:\mathcal{B}_{t},

and the predictable variance proxy for
\mathcal{Z}_{t}=\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=(1-\beta_{t})(G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-g(w_{t}))
evaluates to
\Sigma_{t}^{2}=(1-\beta_{t})^{2}\sigma^{2}
(see Proposition
C.1
and its proof in Appendix
C
).

5.2 Family 2: First-Order Correction

We next consider the first-order differential family, corresponding to the choice
\mathcal{T}_{t}(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t})=0
and its effective innovation is
\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})=(1-\beta_{t})(G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-g(w_{t}))+\beta_{t}\Delta(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t})
. This family includes STORM, PAGE, and SPIDER-type estimators as special cases. The key feature of this recursion is that the deterministic drift from the moving target is removed and replaced by the centered difference noise.

5.2
Sub-Gaussian Lipschitzness of the Centered Differences.

For the analysis of variance-reduced estimators that rely on estimate differences, such as STORM, we require a stronger tail condition on the continuity of the noise. Let
\Delta(w,w^{\prime},\xi)\coloneqq(G(w,\xi)-G(w^{\prime},\xi))-(g(w)-g(w^{\prime}))
denote the centered estimate difference noise. We assume the stochastic oracle is
\ell
-sub-Gaussian Lipschitz, meaning that for all
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\tfrac{\|\Delta(w,w^{\prime},\xi)\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}}{\ell^{2}\|w-w^{\prime}\|^{2}}\right)\right]\leq 2.

Assumption
5.2
is tailored to first-order differential recursions. It ensures that stochastic differences concentrate with a variance proxy proportional to the squared distance between iterates (see two concrete examples for Assumption
5.2
in Subsection
C.5
, Appendix
C
).
Under Assumption
5.2
, the innovation satisfies the conditional regularity condition in Assumption
4.1
. Specifically, this holds with a conditional bias of
\mathcal{B}_{t}=\|\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\|_{\mathcal{G}}=0
and a predictable variance proxy of
\Sigma_{t}^{2}=2(1-\beta_{t})^{2}\sigma^{2}+2\beta_{t}^{2}\ell^{2}\eta_{t-1}^{2}G^{2}
(see Proposition
C.2
and its proof in Appendix
C
).

Specific choices of
(p_{t},\beta_{t})
smoothly recover standard algorithms: STORM when
p_{t}=0
,
\beta_{t}<1
, loopless SVRG/PAGE when
p_{t}\in(0,1)
,
\beta_{t}=1
, and SPIDER/SVRG with periodic resets and
\beta_{t}=1
. In the fully differential regime (
\beta_{t}=1
), the direct-noise term vanishes, leaving only the difference-noise variance proxy
\Sigma_{t}^{2}=2\ell^{2}\eta_{t-1}^{2}G^{2}.

5.3 Family 3: Second-order Correction

We finally consider the second-order family, where the correction term incorporates local curvature information
\mathcal{T}_{t}(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t})\coloneqq\beta_{t}\left(G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t})-\left[G(w_{t},\xi_{t})+\nabla G(w_{t},\xi_{t})(w_{t-1}-w_{t})\right]\right)
, and its effective innovation is
\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})=(1-\beta_{t})(G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-g(w_{t}))+\beta_{t}(g(w_{t-1})-g(w_{t})-\nabla G(w_{t},\xi_{t})(w_{t-1}-w_{t}))
. This correction is designed to remove the leading-order drift term and leave only a second-order Taylor residual, therefore we introduce
L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G})
as the set of all bounded linear operators from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{G}
, and for any
T\in L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G})
and its norm is defined by
\|T\|_{L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G})}:=\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1,x\in\mathcal{X}}\|Tx\|_{\mathcal{G}}
.

5.3
Sub-Gaussian Hessian Noise.

Let
\nabla G(w,\xi)
denote the stochastic operator returned by the oracle such that
\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[\nabla G(w,\xi)]=\nabla g(w)
. We assume the noise in Hessian-vector products is sub-Gaussian. Specifically,

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\|\nabla G(w,\xi)-\nabla g(w)\|_{L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G})}^{2}/\gamma^{2}\right)\right]\leq 2
for any
w\in\mathcal{W}
.

5.4
Smoothness.

g
is
\alpha
-smooth.
That is,

\|\nabla g(w)-\nabla g(w^{\prime})\|_{L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G})}\leq\alpha\|w-w^{\prime}\|
, where
\nabla g
is the Jacobian of
g
, or the Hessian of the objective if
g=\nabla f
, bounded in spectral norm.

Assumptions
5.3
and
5.4
allow us to control the stochastic noise in the Hessian-vector products and bound the second-order Taylor remainder, such that
\|g(w_{t-1})-g(w_{t})-\nabla g(w_{t})(w_{t-1}-w_{t})\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}\|w_{t}-w_{t-1}\|^{2}
. Under these assumptions, the conditional regularity of the innovation (Assumption
4.1
) holds with a conditional bias bounded by
\mathcal{B}_{t}
since

\|\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\|_{\mathcal{G}}=\beta_{t}\|g(w_{t-1})-g(w_{t})-\nabla g(w_{t})(w_{t-1}-w_{t})\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}\beta_{t}\eta_{t-1}^{2}G^{2}=:\mathcal{B}_{t},

and the predictable variance proxy for
\mathcal{Z}_{t}=\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=(1-\beta_{t})(G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-g(w_{t}))-\beta_{t}(\nabla G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-\nabla g(w_{t}))(w_{t-1}-w_{t})
is given by
\Sigma_{t}^{2}=2(1-\beta_{t})^{2}\sigma^{2}+2\beta_{t}^{2}\gamma^{2}\eta_{t-1}^{2}G^{2}
(see Proposition
C.3
and its proof in Appendix
C
).

6  Optimization Guarantees from Unified Estimation

We now show how the unified estimation bound translates into optimization guarantees in two representative settings. The key point is that Theorem
4.2
reduces the analysis to obtaining a uniform high-probability bound on the estimator error. Once such a bound is available, the remaining argument becomes application-specific. In unconstrained stochastic optimization, this yields first-order stationarity guarantees for mirror descent. In expectation-constrained stochastic optimization, it yields feasibility and optimality guarantees for the optimization algorithm.

6.1 Application 1: Stochastic Optimization with Mirror Descent

We consider the stochastic optimization problem (

†

‣
1
) of minimizing
f(w)=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[F(w,\xi)]
over a closed convex set
\mathcal{W}
, where we assume
f
is bounded below such that
\inf_{w\in\mathcal{W}}f(w)>-\infty
. We define the true objective gradient as
g(w):=\nabla f(w)=\nabla\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[F(w,\xi)]
and its unbiased stochastic estimator as
G(w,\xi)
. To relate the unified estimator
v_{t}
of the objective gradient
g(w_{t})
to the update vector
U_{t}
, we select
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}}):=(\mathcal{X}^{*},\|\cdot\|_{*})
and choose
U_{t}=v_{t}/\|v_{t}\|_{*}
as the normalized direction of
v_{t}
. We then perform a mirror descent update
w_{t+1}=w_{t}-\eta_{t}P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})
using a constant step size
\eta_{t}=\eta>0
; since the update vector
\|U_{t}\|_{*}=1
, Assumption
2.3
is satisfied with
G=1
.

Stationarity Criterion.
For any choice of the correction term
\mathcal{T}_{t}
, we measure convergence using the criterion
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\nabla f(w_{t})\|_{\ast}\|P_{t}\|^{2}\leq\varepsilon
for mirror descent with a normalized update vector, where the
proximal stationarity witness
is defined as
P_{t}\coloneqq P\!\left(w_{t},\nabla f(w_{t})/\|\nabla f(w_{t})\|_{\ast},\frac{\eta}{2}\right)
. For an unconstrained Hilbert space (e.g.,
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{X}
) under the
\ell_{2}
-norm, selecting the mirror map
\Phi(w)=\frac{1}{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2}
yields exactly
P(w,u,\eta)=u
. This implies
P_{t}=\nabla f(w_{t})/\|\nabla f(w_{t})\|_{2}
and
\|P_{t}\|=1
, meaning our results with above stationary criterion recover the classic theoretical guarantees for the
\varepsilon
-stationarity of
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\nabla f(w_{t})\|_{2}

[
91
,
33
,
31
,
32
,
86
,
43
]
. For comparison, STORM-type algorithm that uses the gradient estimator directly as the update vector
[
141
]
exhibits a comparable oracle complexity on the order of
\varepsilon^{-3}
, while relying on
\|P(w,\nabla f(w),\eta)\|
as its criterion for first-order stationarity.

Oracle / Iteration Complexities.
For brevity, the results of oracle / iteration complexities for all special cases of these three families are summarized in Table
5
with different selections of
\beta_{t},p_{t}
and
\eta_{t}=\eta
as listed in Table
6
, Appendix
E
, where we define the initial suboptimality gap as
\Delta_{f}\coloneqq f(w_{1})-\inf_{w\in\mathcal{W}}f(w)
, and introduce the following shorthand constants:

\sigma_{L}\coloneqq(\Delta_{f}L)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\sigma_{\ell}\coloneqq(\Delta_{f}\ell)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\sigma_{\gamma}\coloneqq(\Delta_{f}\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\sigma_{\alpha}\coloneqq(\Delta_{f}^{2}\alpha)^{\frac{1}{3}}.

(4)

The parameters
L,\ell,\gamma,
and
\alpha
refer to constants in Assumptions
5.1
,
5.2
,
5.3
, and
5.4
, respectively.

6.1
Complexities, Informal.

Under the parameter selections in Table

, the oracle and iteration complexities
N,T
to achieve
\varepsilon
-stationarity with high probability are:

Family 1:

SGD-M:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\vee\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\big)
;

Probabilistic Momentum:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\big)
;

Periodic Momentum:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\big)
.

Family 2:

STORM:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\big)
;

PAGE:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\big)
;

SPIDER:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\big)
.

Family 3:

SO Momentum:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{\alpha}^{3/2}\sigma}{\varepsilon^{5/2}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\big)
;

SO PAGE:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{\alpha}^{3/2}\sigma}{\varepsilon^{5/2}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\big)
;

SO SPIDER:

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{\alpha}^{3/2}\sigma}{\varepsilon^{5/2}}\vee\frac{\sigma_{L}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\big)
.

We use
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
to hide the dependency on
\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
and
\log\frac{1}{\delta}
in the informal Theorem
6.1
above. It is important to note that our comparisons to existing literature remain valid and fair despite differences in underlying sub-Gaussian assumptions versus bounded expected variance. For Family 1, our complexities for SGD-M match the
\varepsilon^{-4}
rate of existing work that also employs a normalized update vector (see Theorem 1 in
[
31
]
). It allows us to achieve a sharper subdominant
\sigma^{3}/\varepsilon^{3}
term compared to the
(\sigma^{4}/\sigma_{L}^{2})/\varepsilon^{2}+(\sigma_{L}^{2}\sigma^{2})/\varepsilon^{4}
rate established in
[
31
]
, since the latter is strictly lower-bounded by our rate via
2\sqrt{xy}\leq x+y
. While previous works within Family 2 and Family 3 rely on unnormalized update vectors, our approach incorporates normalization; nevertheless, direct comparisons to their respective baselines of complexities remain mathematically fair. Specifically, the complexity rates for STORM align with the dominant
\sigma^{3}/\varepsilon^{3}
term established in existing work (Theorem 1 of
[
33
]
), and the
(\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\sigma)/\varepsilon^{3}
oracle complexity matches previous findings for SPIDER (Theorem 1 in
[
43
]
). For Family 3, which incorporates second-order correction, the oracle complexities of our new second-order variants align with the
(\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\sigma)/\varepsilon^{3}+(\sigma_{\alpha}^{3/2}\sigma)/\varepsilon^{5/2}+\sigma_{L}^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
rate in prior work on variance reduction via Hessian-vector products (Theorem 2, page 8 of
[
6
]
). For details and configurations of our theoretical results, please refer to Tables

,

, as well as the formal Theorem

in Appendix

.

6.2 Application 2: Expectation-Constrained Stochastic Optimization

We now consider the expectation-constrained stochastic optimization problem (

‡

‣
1
), in which feasibility must be determined from noisy observations of the constraint function. Unlike Application 1, where the estimator tracks a gradient used for the update direction, here the estimator tracks the scalar constraint value used for feasibility detection. This setting fits directly into the estimation framework developed in the previous sections by setting

g(w):=h(w),

G(w,\xi):=H(w,\xi),

and hence
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}}):=(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|)
. The unified estimator (

∗

‣
3
) then produces an estimate of
h(w_{t})
, whose error is controlled by Theorem
4.2
. This perspective yields a general reduction from high-probability estimation guarantees to optimization guarantees for constrained stochastic problems. Throughout this subsection, we use a constant step size
\eta_{t}\equiv\eta>0
.

6.2
Constrained Setup.

The functions
f,h
are convex on
\mathcal{W}
. The domain
\mathcal{W}
is bounded such that
\sup_{u,v\in\mathcal{W}}\|u-v\|\leq D
and
\sup_{u,v\in\mathcal{W}}D_{\Phi}(u,v)\leq\frac{R^{2}}{2}
. Furthermore, the stochastic subgradients are conditionally unbiased given the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
and uniformly bounded:

\mathbb{E}[F^{\prime}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\in\partial f(w_{t}),\quad\mathbb{E}[H^{\prime}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\in\partial h(w_{t}),

with
\|F^{\prime}(w,\zeta)\|_{*}\leq G
and
\|H^{\prime}(w,\zeta)\|_{*}\leq G
almost surely for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Moreover, conditioned on the history and the switching decision, the stochastic subgradient used in the update remains unbiased, since
\zeta_{t}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\xi_{t},\xi_{t}^{B}
and
b_{t}
. There exists an optimal solution
w^{\star}\in\arg\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}:\,h(w)\leq 0}f(w)
to (

‡

‣
1
).

Switching Gradient Method (SGM).

To solve (

‡

‣
1
), we use the primal-only
Switching Gradient Method
(SGM)
[
113
,
13
,
83
]
. At iteration
t
, the estimator
v_{t}
is used to decide whether to update using the objective subgradient or the constraint subgradient. Given a switching threshold
\varepsilon>0
, define

U_{t}\coloneqq\mathbb{I}_{\{v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}}\,F^{\prime}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})+\bigl(1-\mathbb{I}_{\{v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}}\bigr)\,H^{\prime}(w_{t},\zeta_{t}),

(5)

and perform mirror-descent
w_{t+1}=\arg\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\left\{\eta\langle U_{t},w\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w,w_{t})\right\}.

Thus, whenever the estimated constraint value is sufficiently small, the method prioritizes descent on the objective; otherwise, it prioritizes reducing constraint violation.

6.3
Constrained SGM via Unified Estimation.

Suppose Assumption
6.2
holds and the unified estimator (

∗

‣
3
) satisfies the uniform tail bound
\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{t\in[T]}|e_{t}|>\mathcal{E}\big)\leq\delta/2
for some deterministic
\mathcal{E}\geq 0
. If we set the switching threshold to

\varepsilon\coloneqq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2DG}{\sqrt{T}}\sqrt{2\log\frac{4}{\delta}}+\mathcal{E},

then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the selected set
\mathcal{A}:=\{t\in[T]:v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}
is non-empty, and the uniform average
\bar{w}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}w_{t}
satisfies both
f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon
and
h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon+\mathcal{E}
.

Theorem
6.3
provides a reusable reduction: once a family-specific estimator yields a high-probability bound
\mathcal{E}
(see instantiations in Table
2
), the optimization guarantee follows by combining this envelope with the SGM error terms from Table
2
. Optimizing the parameters case by case yields the oracle complexities in Table
3
; full statements are given in Corollaries
F.4
–
F.8
in Appendix
F
. The rate is dictated by the estimator envelope
\mathcal{E}
. Family 1 retains a first-order estimation bias and matches the baseline
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-4}\log^{4}(1/\delta))
rate of
[
83
]
, whereas Families 2 and 3 remove this and improve the dominant rate to
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-3})
with milder confidence dependence. In particular, for Families 2 and 3, the
\varepsilon^{-3}
oracle-complexity terms have at most
\log^{3/2}(1/(\varepsilon\delta))
logarithmic confidence dependence in the fully recursive and loopless-reset settings (Cases 1 and 2), whereas the periodic-reset setting (Case 3) exhibits an even tighter logarithmic dependence.
Although Family 1 does not improve the dominant rate, its fully recursive variant
(\beta_{t}=\beta<1,p_{t}=0)
uses batch size
1
throughout, avoiding repeated large-batch constraint evaluations.

7  Conclusion

In this paper, we introduced a unified framework for the design and analysis of recursive stochastic estimators. By decomposing estimator dynamics into memory retention, reset probability, and a dedicated correction term for iterate movement, we provided a lens through which various variance-reduced estimators can be analyzed with high probability.
A main contribution of our work is the development of a unified high-probability estimation bound for general smooth normed spaces. By moving beyond expectation-based and Euclidean-centric analyses, this bound provides robust estimation guarantees for generalized first-order methods, including mirror descent. We demonstrated the power of this approach by recovering high-probability guarantees for unconstrained stochastic optimization and establishing the first variance-reduced oracle complexities for expectation-constrained problems, improving the dominant rate from
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-4})
to
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-3})
.

over

(Definition
2.2
).

.

.

.

\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}}

\kappa

, and

\geq 1
) for the geometry regularity of
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})
.

\xi
, and

.

\xi_{t}

Fresh oracle randomness at time
t
/ “batch” oracle randomness at resets.

v_{t},e_{t}

Unified estimator tracking
g(w_{t})
, estimation error:
e_{t}\coloneqq v_{t}-g(w_{t})
.

L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G}),\|\cdot\|_{L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G})}

L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G})
is the set of all bounded linear operators from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{G}
, and for any
T\in L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G})
, its norm is given by
\|T\|_{L(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{G})}:=\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1,x\in\mathcal{X}}\|Tx\|_{\mathcal{G}}

\mathcal{F}_{t}

Filtration generated by oracle noise and reset events up to time
t
.

b_{t}

Reset indicator:
b_{t}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p_{t})
.

p_{t}

Reset probability (predictable, taking values in
[0,1]
).

\beta_{t}

Momentum parameter (in
[0,1]
), controlling exponential forgetting.

\mathcal{T}_{t}

Correction term used by the unified estimator.

\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})

Effective innovation at time
t
induced by correction term
\mathcal{T}_{t}
.

\Delta_{t}

Difference term in the unified recursion:
\Delta_{t}\coloneqq v_{t-1}-G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t})
.

\Delta(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t})

Centered estimate-difference noise:
\Delta(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t})\coloneqq(G(w_{t},\xi_{t})-G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t}))-(g(w_{t})-g(w_{t-1}))
.

\tau_{m}

m
-th reset time, with
\tau_{0}=0
and
\tau_{m}=\inf\{t>\tau_{m-1}:b_{t}=1\}
.

m(t)

Epoch index such that
t\in(\tau_{m(t)-1},\tau_{m(t)}]
.

\Lambda_{t,j}

Cumulative momentum multiplier:
\Lambda_{t,j}\coloneqq\prod_{i=j+1}^{t}\beta_{i}
,
\Lambda_{t,t}=1
.

A_{t}

Auxiliary multiplier:
A_{t}\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{t}\beta_{i}^{-1}
.

\mathcal{B}_{t}

Conditional bias magnitude of the effective innovation (Assumption
4.1
).

\Sigma_{t}^{2}

Conditional variance proxy for the centered effective innovation (Assumption
4.1
).

\mathfrak{V}_{t}^{2}

Accumulated predictable variance proxy over the current epoch.

V_{t}

Deterministic variance budget such that
\mathfrak{V}_{t}^{2}\leq V_{t}
.

\sigma^{2}

Oracle-noise variance proxy parameter (Assumption
4.1
).

B

Batch/reset parameter used in the reset/initialization concentration term.

T

Deterministic time horizon (w.l.o.g. with
b_{T}=1
).

E

Epoch length for periodic reset schedules (e.g.,
p_{t}=\mathbb{I}_{\{t\ \mathrm{mod}\ E=0\}}
).

Application 1: Stochastic Optimization with Mirror Descent

P_{t}

Proximal stationarity witness at time
t
:
P_{t}\coloneqq P\!\left(w_{t},\frac{\nabla f(w_{t})}{\|\nabla f(w_{t})\|_{\ast}},\frac{\eta}{2}\right)
with
f(w)=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[F(w,\xi)],g(w)=\nabla f(w)=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[G(w,\xi)]
.

\Delta_{f}

Initial objective gap:
\Delta_{f}\coloneqq f(w_{1})-\inf_{w\in\mathcal{W}}f(w)<+\infty
.

\sigma_{L}

Shorthand scale
\sigma_{L}\coloneqq(\Delta_{f}\,L)^{1/2}
, where
L
is from Assumption
5.1
.

\sigma_{\ell}

Shorthand scale
\sigma_{\ell}\coloneqq(\Delta_{f}\,\ell)^{1/2}
, where
\ell
is from Assumption
5.2
.

\sigma_{\gamma}

Shorthand scale
\sigma_{\gamma}\coloneqq(\Delta_{f}\,\gamma)^{1/2}
, where
\gamma
is from Assumption
5.3
.

\sigma_{\alpha}

Shorthand scale
\sigma_{\alpha}\coloneqq(\Delta_{f}^{2}\,\alpha)^{1/3}
, where
\alpha
is from Assumption
5.4
.

Application 2: Expectation-Constrained Stochastic Optimization

F(w,\xi),\,H(w,\xi)

Sample Objective and Constraint functions in the constrained optimization application.

f(w),\,h(w)

Population objective and population constraint:

f(w)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[F(w,\xi)]
,
h(w)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[H(w,\xi)]
.

\zeta_{t}

Fresh stochastic randomness for the stochastic sub-gradients at iteration
t
, which is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\xi_{t},\xi_{t}^{B}
and
b_{t}
.

F^{\prime}(w_{t},\zeta_{t}),\,H^{\prime}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})

Stochastic sub-gradients at iteration
t
, unbiased gradient estimators.

w^{\star}

Any optimal feasible solution of the constrained problem.

\varepsilon

Switching threshold used to decide whether the update prioritizes the objective or the constraint.

\mathcal{A}

Active-phase feasible index set:

\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\{t\in[T]:v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}
.

\mathcal{B}

Active-phase non-feasible index set:

\mathcal{B}\coloneqq[T]\setminus\mathcal{A}
.

\bar{w}

Active-phase selected average:

\bar{w}\coloneqq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}w_{t}
.

\mathcal{E}

Deterministic tail-envelope bound satisfying

|e_{t}|\leq\mathcal{E}
uniformly for all
t\in[T]
on a high-probability event.

\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)

Optimization Error in Theorem
F.1
:

\tfrac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\tfrac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\tfrac{2DG}{\sqrt{T}}\sqrt{2\log\tfrac{4}{\delta}}
.

Banach space (primal norm, dual norm).

The norm
\|\cdot\|
on the reflexive Banach space
\mathcal{X}
is the primal norm used throughout.
Let
\mathcal{X}^{*}
be the dual space, which is also a Banach space. For
u\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, the dual norm is defined by

\|u\|_{\ast}\coloneqq\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1}|u(x)|
. In particular, with the definition of the canonical
duality pairing
\langle u,x\rangle:=u(x)
for
u\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\|u\|_{\ast}=\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1,x\in\mathcal{X}}|\langle u,x\rangle|=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{X}\\
x\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{|\langle u,x\rangle|}{\|x\|},

and hence
\|u\|_{*}\|x\|\geq\langle u,x\rangle
.

A.1
\kappa
-Smoothness for Geometric Regularity.

(see
[
78
, Def. 2.1, p. 2]
)
Let
\kappa\geq 1
and
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})
is a normed space, then
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})
is
\kappa
-smooth if
p:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{R},p(z)\equiv\|z\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2},\forall z\in\mathcal{G}
is continuously differentiable, and for all
z,\Delta\in\mathcal{G}
,

p(z+\Delta)\leq p(z)+\langle\nabla p(z),\Delta\rangle+\kappa p(\Delta).

In particular, for Hilbert spaces we have
\kappa=1
.
For Lebesgue spaces
\mathcal{G}=L_{q}
:

•

If
q\geq 2
, then
\kappa=q-1
.

•

If
q\in(1,2)
, then the dual exponent is
q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{q-1}\in(2,\infty)
, and one typically applies
\kappa
-smoothness to the dual space
\mathcal{G}^{*}=(L_{q})^{*}=L_{q^{\prime}}
, giving
\kappa=q^{\prime}-1=\frac{1}{q-1}
.

•

If
q=1
, then
\mathcal{G}^{*}=L_{\infty}
is not
\kappa
-smooth for any finite
\kappa
in this framework.

A.2
Geometric Regularity.

For a
\kappa
-smooth normed space
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|)
, the
\kappa
-regularity requires the existence of a proxy function
V(\cdot)
with a
\kappa
-Lipschitz gradient
[
78
]
. For the Lebesgue space
L_{q}
with
q\geq 2
, we have
\kappa=q-1
. Furthermore, for Hilbert spaces (e.g.,
d
-dimensional Euclidean space), we have
\kappa=1
.
To extend our analysis to the inherently non-smooth
\ell_{\infty}
geometry,

1
1

1

This is desired as this is the natural space the gradients belong to in the most interesting application of mirror descent methods, i.e., solving problems on the probability simplex, on which the entropy is strongly convex with respect to the
\ell_{1}
norm.

we utilize a smooth
\ell_{q}
approximation. Given a primal space
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\|\cdot\|_{p})
with
p=1+\varepsilon
and its conjugate
q=1+1/\varepsilon
, the regularity constant scales as
\kappa=q-1=1/\varepsilon
. Notably let
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{\ln(\max\{e,d\})}
which yields
\kappa=\ln(\max\{e,d\})
, providing a dimension-insensitive deviation radius of order
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ln d})
, while enforcing
\kappa=1
when
d\leq 2
.

Mirror map and Bregman divergence.

A.3
Mirror Map.

The mirror map
\Phi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
with
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
is proper, weakly lower semi-continuous (w.l.s.c), and
1
-strongly convex on a nonempty closed convex set
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\text{int}(\mathcal{D})
with respect to
\|\cdot\|
. Furthermore,
\Phi
is Gâteaux differentiable on
\mathcal{W}
.

Its Bregman divergence
D_{\Phi}(x,y)
which is defined below satisfies the inequality for all
x,y\in\mathcal{W}
:

D_{\Phi}(x,y)\coloneqq\Phi(x)-\Phi(y)-\langle\nabla\Phi(y),x-y\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}.

Then we have the following inequality for all
x,y\in\mathcal{W}
:

D_{\Phi}(x,y)+D_{\Phi}(y,x)=\langle\nabla\Phi(x)-\nabla\Phi(y),x-y\rangle\geq\|x-y\|^{2}.

A.4
Proximal Gradient Mapping.

For
w\in\mathcal{W}
, an input vector
u\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, and a step size
\eta>0
, the
proximal gradient mapping
is

P(w,u,\eta)=(w-w^{+})/\eta
,
where the updated point
w^{+}
is the unique minimizer of the proximal subproblem:

w^{+}=\arg\min_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}}\langle\eta u,w^{\prime}\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)

and
D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)
denotes the Bregman divergence induced by
\Phi
.

A.5
First-Order Sufficient/Necessary Optimality Condition.

Because the proximal objective
\mathcal{L}(w^{\prime};w,u,\eta)=\langle\eta u,w^{\prime}\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)
is strictly convex and Gâteaux differentiable, and the domain
\mathcal{W}
is convex, the variational inequality below is both a necessary and sufficient condition for global optimality. The necessity follows from the non-negativity of the directional derivative along feasible segments, while the sufficiency follows directly from the first-order characterization of convexity, which guarantees
\mathcal{L}(w^{\prime};w,u,\eta)\geq\mathcal{L}(w^{+};w,u,\eta)+\langle\nabla\mathcal{L}(w^{+};w,u,\eta),w^{\prime}-w^{+}\rangle\geq\mathcal{L}(w^{+};w,u,\eta)
for all
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
.

\langle\nabla\mathcal{L}(w^{+};w,u,\eta),\,w^{\prime}-w^{+}\rangle=\left\langle\eta u+\big(\nabla\Phi(w^{+})-\nabla\Phi(w)\big),\,w^{\prime}-w^{+}\right\rangle\geq 0.

By applying the Bregman Three-Point Identity, we have:

\langle\nabla\Phi(w^{+})-\nabla\Phi(w),w^{\prime}-w^{+}\rangle=D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)-D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w^{+})-D_{\Phi}(w^{+},w)

Substituting the above into the first-order optimality condition, we obtain the equivalent condition:

\eta\langle u,w^{\prime}-w^{+}\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)-D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w^{+})-D_{\Phi}(w^{+},w)\geq 0.

For Mirror Descent, the update rule with some update vector
U_{t}
and step size
\eta_{t}
is given by

w_{t+1}=\underset{w\in\mathcal{W}}{\arg\min}\left\{\langle U_{t},w\rangle+\frac{1}{\eta_{t}}D_{\Phi}(w,w_{t})\right\}=P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})

A.6
Properties of Proximal Gradient Mapping, see Lemmas 1-3 in
[
46
]
for first two properties.

For any
w\in\mathcal{W}
, input vector
u\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, and step size
\eta>0
, the proximal gradient mapping
P(w,u,\eta)
has the following properties:

•

(Inner Product Bounds) For any
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\|P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\langle u_{1}-u_{2},P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{\ast}\|P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\|.

In particular,
P(w,0,\eta)=0
from the definition of proximal gradient mapping, therefore

\displaystyle\|P(w,u,\eta)\|^{2}\leq\langle u,P(w,u,\eta)\rangle\leq\|u\|_{\ast}\|P(w,u,\eta)\|.

•

(Non-expansiveness) For any
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
,

\|P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\|\leq\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{\ast}.

In particular,
P(w,0,\eta)=0
from the definition of proximal gradient mapping, therefore

\|P(w,u,\eta)\|=\|P(w,u,\eta)\|\leq\|u\|_{\ast}.

•

(Scaling Property) For any scalar
\alpha>0
,

P(w,\alpha u,\eta)=\alpha P(w,u,\alpha\eta).

•

(Monotonicity) For any
\eta^{\prime}>\eta>0
,

\langle u,\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})\rangle\geq\langle u,\eta P(w,u,\eta)\rangle+\frac{\|\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})-\eta P(w,u,\eta)\|^{2}}{\eta^{\prime}-\eta}.

Therefore,
\langle u,\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})\rangle\geq\langle u,\eta P(w,u,\eta)\rangle
and:

\|\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})-\eta P(w,u,\eta)\|\leq(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)\|u\|_{\ast}.

Proof.

Proof of the inner product bounds:

We establish the lower bound of
\langle u_{1}-u_{2},P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\rangle
by
applying the first-order optimality condition discussed in Remark on Definition
2.2

and using the fact
D_{\Phi}(x,y)+D_{\Phi}(y,x)\geq\|x-y\|^{2}
for the 1-strongly convex mirror map
\Phi
.

\eta\langle u,w^{\prime}-w^{+}\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)-D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w^{+})-D_{\Phi}(w^{+},w)\geq 0.

By introducing
w^{+}_{1}:=\arg\min_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}}\langle\eta u_{1},w^{\prime}\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)
,

w^{+}_{2}:=\arg\min_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}}\langle\eta u_{2},w^{\prime}\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)
, therefore

w^{+}_{1}=w-\eta P(w,u_{1},\eta),w^{+}_{2}=w-\eta P(w,u_{2},\eta)
,
and letting
u\leftarrow u_{1},w^{+}\leftarrow w^{+}_{1},w^{\prime}\leftarrow w^{+}_{2}
and
u\leftarrow u_{2},w^{+}\leftarrow w^{+}_{2},w^{\prime}\leftarrow w^{+}_{1}

in the first-order optimality condition above:

\eta^{2}\langle u_{1},P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\rangle\geq-D_{\Phi}(w^{+}_{2},w)+D_{\Phi}(w^{+}_{2},w^{+}_{1})+D_{\Phi}(w^{+}_{1},w)

\eta^{2}\langle u_{2},P(w,u_{2},\eta)-P(w,u_{1},\eta)\rangle\geq-D_{\Phi}(w^{+}_{1},w)+D_{\Phi}(w^{+}_{1},w^{+}_{2})+D_{\Phi}(w^{+}_{2},w)

Adding the above two inequalities, and applying
D_{\Phi}(x,y)+D_{\Phi}(y,x)\geq\|x-y\|^{2}

for all
x,y\in\mathcal{W}
and 1-strongly convex mirror map
\Phi
, we obtain:

\eta^{2}\langle u_{1}-u_{2},P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\rangle\geq\|w^{+}_{1}-w^{+}_{2}\|^{2}=\eta^{2}\|P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\|^{2}.

Therefore, since
\eta>0
, we establish the lower bound of
\langle u_{1}-u_{2},P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\rangle
:

\langle u_{1}-u_{2},P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\rangle\geq\|P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\|^{2}.

From the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{*}
, we have
\langle u,x\rangle\leq\|u\|_{*}\|x\|
for all
x\in\mathcal{X},u\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
. Therefore, we establish the upper bound of
\langle u_{1}-u_{2},P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\rangle
:

\langle u_{1}-u_{2},P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\rangle\leq\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{\ast}\|P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\|.

Proof of the non-expansiveness:

Combining the lower and upper bounds, and noting that
x^{2}\leq xy
for some
x,y\geq 0
implies
x\leq y
,
we obtain the desired non-expansiveness of the proximal gradient mapping:

\|P(w,u_{1},\eta)-P(w,u_{2},\eta)\|\leq\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{\ast}.

Proof of the scaling property:

The scaling property of the proximal gradient mapping follows from its definition,
and noting that
\eta\cdot\alpha u=\alpha\eta\cdot u
.

Proof of the monotonicity:

For any
\eta^{\prime}>\eta\geq 0
,
u\in\mathcal{X}^{\ast},w\in\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
,
we introduce
w^{+}:=\arg\min_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}}\langle\eta u,w^{\prime}\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)
,

w^{\prime+}:=\arg\min_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}}\langle\eta^{\prime}u,w^{\prime}\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime},w)
,
therefore
w^{+}=w-\eta P(w,u,\eta),w^{{}^{\prime}+}=w-\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})
,
and letting
w^{\prime}\leftarrow w^{\prime+}
and
\eta\leftarrow\eta^{\prime},w^{+}\leftarrow w^{\prime+},w^{\prime}\leftarrow w^{+}
in the first-order optimality condition:

\eta\langle u,\eta P(w,u,\eta)-\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})\rangle\geq-D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime+},w)+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime+},w^{+})+D_{\Phi}(w^{+},w)

\eta^{\prime}\langle u,\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})-\eta P(w,u,\eta)\rangle\geq-D_{\Phi}(w^{+},w)+D_{\Phi}(w^{+},w^{\prime+})+D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime+},w)

Adding the above two inequalities and dividing by
\eta^{\prime}-\eta>0
, then
appyling
D_{\Phi}(x,y)+D_{\Phi}(y,x)\geq\|x-y\|^{2}
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{W}
and 1-strongly convex mirror map
\Phi
, we obtain:

\langle u,\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})-\eta P(w,u,\eta)\rangle\geq\frac{D_{\Phi}(w^{\prime+},w^{+})+D_{\Phi}(w^{+},w^{\prime+})}{\eta^{\prime}-\eta}\geq\frac{\|\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})-\eta P(w,u,\eta)\|^{2}}{\eta^{\prime}-\eta}

By
\langle u,\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})-\eta P(w,u,\eta)\rangle\leq\|u\|_{\ast}\|\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})-\eta P(w,u,\eta)\|

from the definition of the dual norm
\|\cdot\|_{\ast}
, we have:

\|\eta^{\prime}P(w,u,\eta^{\prime})-\eta P(w,u,\eta)\|\leq(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)\|u\|_{\ast}.

∎

Unified estimator
v_{t}
and estimation error
e_{t}
.

For a target
g(w)
and a stochastic oracle
G(w,\xi)
with

\mathbb{E}_{\xi}[G(w,\xi)]=g(w)

which is an unbiased estimator of the target function
g(w)
.
We introduce a unified estimator
v_{t}
to track the target function value
g(w_{t})
at the
t
-th iteration,
by using
G(w,\xi)
and the unbiased estimator of gradient
\nabla G(w,\xi)
such that
\mathbb{E}[\nabla G(w,\xi)]=\nabla g(w)
to reduce the variance of estimation error.

At each iteration
t
, let the reset indicator be
b_{t}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p_{t})
,
where
p_{t}\in[0,1]
is the reset probability
(possibly adaptive and predictable; see Assumption
3.1
).
When
b_{t}=1
, the estimator uses an independent “batch” oracle randomness
\xi_{t}^{B}:=(\xi_{t}^{(i)})_{i=1}^{B}
from the oracle,
then reset the estimator
v_{t}
to the average of evaluated function values
G(w_{t},\xi_{t}^{(i)}),i=1,\dots,B
,
with
B
samples
\xi_{t}^{B}:=(\xi_{t}^{(i)})_{i=1}^{B}
.
When
b_{t}=0
, the estimator uses a single independent sample
\xi_{t}
drawn from the oracle,
to evaluate function value
G(w,\xi)
or gradient value
\nabla G(w,\xi)
at
w_{t},w_{t-1}
with the same sample
\xi_{t}
,
and then update the estimator
v_{t}
by using the evaluated function/gradient value and the previous estimator
v_{t-1}

to track the target function value
g(w_{t})
.

The unified estimator is specified by momentum parameters
\{\beta_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
with
\beta_{t}\in[0,1]
and by
a correction term
\Delta_{t}
and
a correction term
\mathcal{T}_{t}
as follows:

v_{t}:=\begin{cases}G(w_{t},\xi_{t}^{B}),&b_{t}=1,\\
G(w_{t},\xi_{t})+\beta_{t}\Delta_{t}+\mathcal{T}_{t},&b_{t}=0,\end{cases}

(6)

where
\Delta_{t}\coloneqq v_{t-1}-G(w_{t-1},\xi_{t})
,

\mathcal{T}_{t}\coloneqq\mathcal{T}_{t}(w_{t},w_{t-1},\xi_{t})

depends on the evaluated function value
G(w,\xi)
and estimated gradient value
\nabla G(w,\xi)
at
w_{t},w_{t-1}
with the same sample
\xi_{t}
,
and
G(w_{t},\xi_{t}^{B})\coloneqq\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}G(w_{t},\xi_{t}^{(i)})

is the average of evaluated function values
G(w_{t},\xi_{t}^{(i)}),i=1,\dots,B
.
At the first iteration
t=1
, we set
\Delta_{1}=0,\mathcal{T}_{1}=0
by definition.

The tracking error
e_{t}
between the unified estimator
v_{t}
and the target function value
g(w_{t})
is given by

e_{t}\coloneqq v_{t}-g(w_{t}).

Filtration, resets/epochs, and cumulative momentum multipliers.

For high-probability analysis, let
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the filtration generated by all oracle noise and reset events up to time
t
.

\mathcal{F}_{0}=\Omega,\quad\mathcal{F}_{t}=\begin{cases}\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{t-1},b_{t},\xi_{t}\big|_{b_{t}=0},\xi_{t}^{B}\big|_{b_{t}=1})&\text{for Application 1\penalty\ \eqref{eq:application1}},\\
\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{t-1},b_{t},\xi_{t}\big|_{b_{t}=0},\xi_{t}^{B}\big|_{b_{t}=1},\zeta_{t})&\text{for Application 2\penalty\ \eqref{eq:application2}}.\end{cases}.

Let the random sequence of epoch boundaries
\{\tau_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
for
m
-th reset time be defined by

\tau_{0}=0,\qquad\tau_{m}=\inf\{t>\tau_{m-1}:b_{t}=1\}\quad(1\leq m\leq T),

where
T
is a deterministic horizon with
b_{T}=1
(w.l.o.g.). For each
t\in\{1,\dots,T\}
, let
m(t)
be the unique index such that
t\in(\tau_{m(t)-1},\tau_{m(t)}]
.

For the momentum parameters
\{\beta_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
with
\beta_{t}\in[0,1]
,
we define the cumulative momentum multipliers for
j=0,\dots,t-1
as follows:

\Lambda_{t,j}\coloneqq\prod_{i=j+1}^{t}\beta_{i},\qquad\Lambda_{t,t}=1.

We also introduce
A_{t}\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{t}\beta_{i}^{-1}
and its inverse
A_{t}^{-1}=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\beta_{i}
to simplify the notations.

Oracle noise variance proxy.

We also use the oracle noise variance proxy
\sigma^{2}>0
and a (positive) reset/batch parameter
B
in
\xi_{t}^{B}
and
\xi_{t}
, see also Assumption
4.1
.
We also introduce the conditional bias magnitude
\mathcal{B}_{t}
and variance proxy
\Sigma_{t}^{2}

for the effective innovation
\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})
(see Definition
3.3
) as shown in Assumption
4.1
to simplify the notations:

\mathcal{B}_{t}\geq\big\|\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\big\|_{\mathcal{G}},\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\|\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{t}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}}{\Sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\leq\exp(1).

For the accumulated predictable variance proxy on the current epoch, we introduce the notation:

\mathfrak{V}_{t}^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{j=\tau_{m(t)-1}+1}^{t}A_{j}^{2}\Sigma_{j}^{2},

which is bounded by a deterministic variance budget
V_{t}\geq 0

such that
\mathfrak{V}_{t}^{2}\leq V_{t}
as shown in Theorem
B.2
and Main Theorem
4.2
.

Appendix B
Proofs for Results of Unified Estimation Error Bound

B.1 Lemma of Masked Martingale Difference Sequence

B.1
Masked Martingale Difference Sequence.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space equipped with a filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
. Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a martingale difference sequence adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{n})
, satisfying
\mathbb{E}[|X_{n}|]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}[X_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]=0
almost surely.
Let
S
and
T
be two stopping times with respect to
(\mathcal{F}_{n})
such that
S\leq T
almost surely. Define the masked sequence
(Y_{n})_{n\geq 1}
as

Y_{n}\coloneqq X_{n}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{S<n\leq T\}}.

(7)

Then,
(Y_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a martingale difference sequence with respect to
(\mathcal{F}_{n})
. Consequently, for any
N\geq 1
, the sum over the random interval corresponds to the sum of the masked sequence

\sum_{n=S+1}^{T}X_{n}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}Y_{n}.

(8)

Proof.

To establish that
(Y_{n})_{n\geq 1}
constitutes a martingale difference sequence, we must demonstrate that
Y_{n}
is integrable, adapted to
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, and satisfies the zero conditional mean property. We begin by examining the indicator process
H_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{I}_{\{S<n\leq T\}}
. The event
\{S<n\leq T\}
can be decomposed as the intersection
\{S\leq n-1\}\cap\{T\leq n-1\}^{c}
. By the definition of a stopping time, the event
\{S\leq n-1\}
is
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
-measurable. Similarly,
\{T\leq n-1\}\in\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
, which implies its complement is also in
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
is a
\sigma
-algebra, the intersection of these events is
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
-measurable.
This establishes that the process
(H_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is predictable. Since
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{n}
, the variable
H_{n}
is also
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-measurable. As
X_{n}
is adapted to
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, the product
Y_{n}=X_{n}H_{n}
is
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-measurable and integrable, satisfying
\mathbb{E}[|Y_{n}|]\leq\mathbb{E}[|X_{n}|]<\infty
.
We proceed to compute the conditional expectation of
Y_{n}
given the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}

\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{n}\cdot H_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}].

(9)

Because
H_{n}
is
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
-measurable, it acts as a constant with respect to the conditional expectation and can be factored out. This yields

\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]=H_{n}\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}].

(10)

By hypothesis,
(X_{n})
is a martingale difference sequence, so
\mathbb{E}[X_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]=0
almost surely. Therefore,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]=0
. This confirms that
(Y_{n})
is a martingale difference sequence. The equality of the summations follows directly from the definition of the indicator function, which takes the value
1
when the index
n
falls between the stopping times
S
and
T
, and
0
otherwise.
∎

B.2 Theorem of Vector-Valued Freedman Inequality

B.2
Vector-Valued Freedman Inequality.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})
be a
\kappa
-smooth normed space. Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
be a filtered probability space, and let
\{Y_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
be an
\mathcal{X}
-valued martingale difference sequence adapted to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
. Assume there exists a sequence of non-negative predictable random variables
\{\Sigma_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
, where each
\Sigma_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable. Assume that on the
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable event
\{\Sigma_{t}=0\}
,
Y_{t}=0
almost surely.
2
2

2

We adopt the analytic convention that
0/0=0
; such a requirement is essential for our ensuing assumptions on sub-Gaussian notions of Lipschitzness where the variance proxy includes terms such as
\|w-w^{\prime}\|
in its denominator.
Assume that the conditional light-tail bound evaluates to

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}}{\Sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\leq 2,

(11)

almost surely for all
t\geq 1
. Define the accumulated predictable variance proxy as
\mathfrak{V}_{t}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{t}\Sigma_{j}^{2}
, and let
M_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{t}Y_{j}
denote the martingale. For any deterministic variance budget
V>0
and any deviation parameter
\gamma\geq 0
, the following high-probability bound holds for all
t\geq 1
:

\mathbb{P}\left(\|M_{t}\|_{\mathcal{G}}\geq\left(\sqrt{\kappa}+\gamma\right)\sqrt{V}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{V}_{t}^{2}\leq V\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{3}\right).

(12)

Hence, for any deterministic variance budget
V>0
and any target confidence level
\delta\in(0,1)
, the following concentration bound holds for all
t\geq 1
:

\mathbb{P}\left(\|M_{t}\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C(\delta,\kappa)\sqrt{V}\right)\geq 1-\delta-\mathbb{P}\left(\mathfrak{V}_{t}^{2}>V\right),

(13)

where
C(\delta,\kappa):=\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{3\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}
.

Geometric Implications.
Let us first remark. Note that the
(2,D)
-smoothness of Pinelis
[
112
]
and the
\kappa
-regularity of Juditsky and Nemirovski
[
78
]
are connected via
\kappa=D^{2}
(see the definition of
\kappa
-smoothness in Appendix
A
). Specifically, for a normed space
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})
,
[
78
]
requires the existence of a proxy function
V(\cdot)
with a
\kappa
-Lipschitz gradient, whereas
[
112
]
defines smoothness through the inequality
\|x+y\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}+\|x-y\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\leq 2\|x\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}+2D^{2}\|y\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}
. For the Lebesgue space
L_{q}
with
q\geq 2
, both frameworks converge on the optimal regularity parameter
\kappa=q-1
. Furthermore, for Hilbert spaces (e.g.,
d
-dimensional Euclidean space), we have
\kappa=D=1
. Further discussion is deferred to the Appendix
A
.

Proof.

Following standard concentration techniques for martingales with predictable variance sequences, e.g.
[
112
]
, we define the stopping time
\tau
with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
as

\tau=\inf\left\{k\geq 0:\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}\Sigma_{j}^{2}>V\right\},

(14)

where
\inf\emptyset=\infty
(indicating the ideal case). The equivalence
\{\tau\leq t-1\}=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{t}\Sigma_{j}^{2}>V\right\}
guarantees the event is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable, ensuring
\tau
is a valid stopping time. We subsequently analyze the stopped martingale sequence
M_{t\wedge\tau}
, where
t\wedge\tau
is a shorthand for
\min(t,\tau)
.

We now follow the approach of
[
78
]
to construct a scalar supermartingale utilizing a proxy function mapping derived from the dual geometry of
\mathcal{G}
. Let
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}
denote the dual norm. We define the base proxy function
V:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{R}
via the Legendre transform

V(\xi)=\sup_{\|\eta\|_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}\leq 1}\left[\langle\xi,\eta\rangle-\frac{\kappa}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}^{2}\right].

(15)

Because the space
\mathcal{G}
is
\kappa
-smooth, the dual penalty
v(\eta)=\frac{\kappa}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}^{2}
is globally
1
-strongly convex (Page 22 of
[
78
]
). Standard convex analysis (Page 22 of
[
78
]
) establishes that
V(\cdot)
is continuously differentiable, its gradient satisfies
\|V^{\prime}(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}\leq 1
, and
V^{\prime}
is globally
1
-Lipschitz continuous, ensuring

V(\xi+\Delta)\leq V(\xi)+\langle V^{\prime}(\xi),\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\|\Delta\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}.

(16)

Further, the primal geometric offset is bounded by

\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{G}}=\sup_{\|\eta\|_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}\leq 1}\langle\xi,\eta\rangle\leq\frac{\kappa}{2}+V(\xi).

(17)

Following the proof of Theorem 4.1 in
[
78
]
, for any fixed scalar
\beta>0
(to be optimized later), the scaled proxy function
V_{\beta}(\xi)=\beta V(\xi/\beta)
preserves the gradient bound and modifies the spatial constraints to

\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{\kappa\beta}{2}+V_{\beta}(\xi)\quad\text{and}\quad V_{\beta}(\xi+\Delta)\leq V_{\beta}(\xi)+\langle V_{\beta}^{\prime}(\xi),\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{2\beta}\|\Delta\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}.

(18)

By defining the scalar increments
\psi_{i}=V_{\beta}(M_{i})-V_{\beta}(M_{i-1})
, the condition
\|V_{\beta}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{G}^{*}}\leq 1
(i.e.
V_{\beta}
having a Lipschitz constant of 1) restricts the sequence to
|\psi_{i}|\leq\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathcal{G}}
. The Lipschitz continuity of
V_{\beta}^{\prime}
with constant
(1/\beta)
and
M_{i}=M_{i-1}+Y_{i}
yields

\psi_{i}=V_{\beta}(M_{i})-V_{\beta}(M_{i-1})\leq\langle V_{\beta}^{\prime}(M_{i-1}),Y_{i}\rangle+\frac{1}{2\beta}\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2},

(19)

such that after taking the expectation and noting
Y_{i}
is a martingale difference

\mathbb{E}[\psi_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq\frac{1}{2\beta}\mathbb{E}[\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}].

(20)

Applying the inequality
1+x\leq\exp(x)
at
x=\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}/\Sigma_{i}^{2}
provides
\mathbb{E}[\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq\Sigma_{i}^{2}
. Consequently, the predictable drift is bounded by

\mathbb{E}[\psi_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq\mu_{i}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\beta}\Sigma_{i}^{2}.

(21)

To bound the exponential moments, the condition
|\psi_{i}|\leq\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathcal{G}}
and Eq. (
11
) enforce
\mathbb{E}[\exp(\psi_{i}^{2}/\Sigma_{i}^{2})\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq 2
. Using the inequality
\exp(s)\leq s+\exp(9s^{2}/16)
which holds for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and Eq. (
21
), we evaluate the moment generating function for any
\lambda\geq 0

\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\psi_{i})\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq\lambda\mu_{i}+\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{9\lambda^{2}\psi_{i}^{2}}{16}\right)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right].

(22)

As standard in deriving Bernstein-type inequalities or uniform sub-Gaussian bounds, we need to handle two cases to separately deal with convex and concave cases. We first consider the latter, which corresponds to
0\leq\lambda\leq\frac{4}{3\Sigma_{i}}
. Defining
\theta=\frac{9\lambda^{2}\Sigma_{i}^{2}}{16}\in[0,1]
and applying Jensen’s inequality to the concave function
z\mapsto z^{\theta}
yields
\mathbb{E}[\exp(\psi_{i}^{2}/\Sigma_{i}^{2})^{\theta}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq\exp(\theta)
. Because
\lambda\mu_{i}
and
9\lambda^{2}\Sigma_{i}^{2}/16
are non-negative,
x+\exp(y)\leq\exp(x+y)
establishes the majorant
\exp(\lambda\mu_{i}+\frac{3}{4}\lambda^{2}\Sigma_{i}^{2})
.

For
\lambda>\frac{4}{3\Sigma_{i}}
(the convex case), applying Young’s inequality
\lambda x\leq\frac{3}{8}\lambda^{2}\Sigma_{i}^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{x^{2}}{\Sigma_{i}^{2}}
at
x=\psi_{i}
gives

\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\psi_{i})\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq\exp\left(\frac{3}{8}\lambda^{2}\Sigma_{i}^{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{2}{3}\frac{\psi_{i}^{2}}{\Sigma_{i}^{2}}\right)\middle|\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right].

(23)

Now we can apply Jensen’s inequality for the exponent
2/3\in[0,1]
to bound the inner expectation by
\exp(2/3)
. The domain constraint necessitates
\frac{3}{8}\lambda^{2}\Sigma_{i}^{2}>\frac{2}{3}
, ensuring
\exp(2/3)\leq\exp(\frac{3}{8}\lambda^{2}\Sigma_{i}^{2})
. Thus, the uniform bound

\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\psi_{i})\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq\exp(\lambda\mu_{i}+\frac{3}{4}\lambda^{2}\Sigma_{i}^{2})

(24)

holds globally for all
\lambda
. Furthermore, integrating the stopping time, we define the masked variables

\tilde{\psi}_{i}=\psi_{i}\mathbb{I}_{\{i\leq\tau\}},\quad\tilde{\mu}_{i}=\mu_{i}\mathbb{I}_{\{i\leq\tau\}},\quad\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}^{2}=\Sigma_{i}^{2}\mathbb{I}_{\{i\leq\tau\}}.

(25)

Since
\{i\leq\tau\}
is
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
-measurable, the conditional expectation bound in Eq. (
24
) is preserved , i.e.
3
3

3

To see this, note that on event
\{t\leq\tau\}
the indicator evaluates to
I_{t}=1
and (
26
) reduces to (
24
) while on event
\{t>\tau\}
both sides equate to 1 as the indicator evaluates to
I_{t}=0
.

\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda\tilde{\psi}_{t})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq\exp(\lambda\tilde{\mu}_{t}+\frac{3}{4}\lambda^{2}\tilde{\Sigma}_{t}^{2}).

(26)

To construct the unconditional bound, we define the variance-penalized process

Z_{t}=\exp\left(\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\psi}_{i}-\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\mu}_{i}-\frac{3}{4}\lambda^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}^{2}\right).

(27)

To verify that
Z_{t}
is a supermartingale adapted to
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, we isolate the terminal increment following an approach similar to Lemma 20.2 in
[
84
]
, i.e.

Z_{t}=Z_{t-1}\exp\left(\lambda\tilde{\psi}_{t}-\lambda\tilde{\mu}_{t}-\frac{3}{4}\lambda^{2}\tilde{\Sigma}_{t}^{2}\right).

(28)

Because
\tau
is a predictable stopping time,
\tilde{\mu}_{t}
and
\tilde{\Sigma}_{t}^{2}
are
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable. Factoring these out of the conditional expectation yields

\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=Z_{t-1}\exp\left(-\lambda\tilde{\mu}_{t}-\frac{3}{4}\lambda^{2}\tilde{\Sigma}_{t}^{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(\lambda\tilde{\psi}_{t})\middle|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right].

(29)

Substituting the bound from Eq. (
26
) cancels the variance penalty terms, establishing
\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq Z_{t-1}
. Thus, by tower expectation,
\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Z_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]]\leq\mathbb{E}[Z_{k-1}]
, and doing so iteratively yields
\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}]\leq\mathbb{E}[Z_{0}]=1
.

By definitions in Eq. (
14
) and Eq. (
25
), the cumulative constraints
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}^{2}\leq V
and
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\mu}_{i}\leq\frac{1}{2\beta}V
hold deterministically. On the event
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\psi}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{2\beta}V+r
, the supermartingale ensures
Z_{t}\geq\exp(\lambda r-\frac{3}{4}\lambda^{2}V)
. Markov’s inequality yields

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\psi}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{2\beta}V+r\right)\leq\exp\left(-\lambda r+\frac{3}{4}\lambda^{2}V\right).

(30)

Minimizing the quadratic exponent at
\lambda=\frac{2r}{3V}
produces the optimal limit
\exp(-\frac{r^{2}}{3V})
.

On the intersection event where
\mathfrak{V}_{t}^{2}\leq V
, we have
\tau\geq t
, and thus
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\psi}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\psi_{i}
.

Thus, using the definition
\psi_{i}=V_{\beta}(M_{i})-V_{\beta}(M_{i-1})
and a telescoping sum we have
V_{\beta}(M_{t})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\psi}_{i}
. Utilizing the first part of Eq. (
18
), the norm satisfies

\|M_{t}\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{\kappa\beta}{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tilde{\psi}_{i}\leq\frac{\kappa\beta}{2}+\frac{1}{2\beta}V+r.

(31)

Optimizing with respect to
\beta
via differentiation yields the unique minimum
\beta=\sqrt{\frac{V}{\kappa}}
. Substituting this parameter establishes

\frac{\kappa}{2}\sqrt{\frac{V}{\kappa}}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\kappa V}=\sqrt{\kappa V}.

(32)

Parameterizing the deviation in Eq. (
30
) as
r=\gamma\sqrt{V}
, the deviation simplifies to
(\sqrt{\kappa}+\gamma)\sqrt{V}
, and the corresponding tail probability evaluates to
\exp(-\gamma^{2}/3)
, thereby furnishing the proof.
∎

B.3
Optimization Implications.

The formulation of Theorem
B.2
exhibits a strict topological decoupling between the martingale geometry and the variance proxy constructions. The
\kappa
-smoothness condition is imposed exclusively on the space
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})
governing the martingale difference sequence
Y_{t}
. The predictable variance proxy
\Sigma_{t}
operates purely as an
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable scalar bound. Consequently, if
\Sigma_{t}
is parameterized by auxiliary algorithmic quantities, e.g.,
\|U\|_{q}
or
\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{r}
for arbitrary
q,r\in[1,\infty]
, the probabilistic concentration mechanism remains completely agnostic to these auxiliary topologies. However, integrating this concentration into the ensuing application of optimization analysis imposes certain structural constraints on the permissible
\ell_{p}
norms, dictated entirely by the nature of the stochastic sequence:

•

Concentration of Gradients (Typical Application):
If the martingale models stochastic gradient residuals, the sequence inherently resides in the topological dual space,
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{X}^{*}
. To invoke the theorem natively without dimension-dependent distortion,
\mathcal{X}^{*}
must be
\kappa
-smooth, which necessitates an
\ell_{p}
norm geometry where
p\in[2,\infty)
. By strict Hölder conjugacy (
1/p+1/q=1
), the primal iterate space
\mathcal{X}
is formally restricted to an
\ell_{q}
geometry where
q\in(1,2]
.

•

Concentration of Function Values (SGM Application):
If the martingale models scalar stochastic noise in the objective evaluations, the sequence resides in
\mathcal{G}=\mathbb{R}
. The absolute value norm constitutes a trivially
1
-smooth Hilbert geometry (
\kappa=1
). The resulting concentration is unconditionally dimension-free. Under this regime, the primal iterate space
\mathcal{W}
may be equipped with any arbitrary norm without inducing geometric penalties, as the concentration bound does not interact with the dual pairing of the algorithmic step.

B.3 Proof for Theorem
4.2
of Unified Estimation Error Bound

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Let
\tau_{m}
denote the sequence of resets, defined recursively as
\tau_{0}=0
, and
\tau_{m}=\inf\{t>\tau_{m-1}:b_{t}=1\}
for
1\leq m\leq T
. Note that
\tau_{m}
is a valid stopping time since
b_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable by Assumption
3.1
. By the condition that the oracle noise is conditionally sub-Gaussian with
\sigma^{2}
, we obtain that with probability at least
1-\delta/2
by applying the established Theorem
B.2
and using Assumption
3.2
, the error at the initialization and reset iterations satisfies

\|e_{\tau_{m-1}}\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C\left(\frac{\delta}{2},\kappa\right)\sigma/\sqrt{B}.

(33)

Let us fix a specific epoch index
m\geq 1
and consider the temporal index
