Appendix F
Application 2: Expectation-Constrained Stochastic Optimization

Optimization Problem

We aim to solve the stochastic constrained optimization problem:

\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}f(w)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[F(w,\xi)]\quad\text{s.t.}\quad h(w)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[H(w,\xi)]\leq 0,

where
F(w,\xi):\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
and
H(w,\xi):\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
are convex,
\mathcal{W}
is a closed, convex subset of
\mathcal{X}
.

The Unified Estimator and SGM Update Rule

•

Update Direction:

U_{t}\coloneqq\mathbb{I}_{\{v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}}F^{{}^{\prime}}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})+(1-\mathbb{I}_{\{v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}})H^{{}^{\prime}}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})
.

•

Mirror Descent Step:

w_{t+1}=\underset{w\in\mathcal{W}}{\arg\min}\left\{\langle\eta_{t}U_{t},w\rangle+D_{\Phi}(w,w_{t})\right\}=w_{t}-\eta_{t}P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})

where
\eta_{t}=\eta
is the constant step size.

The active-phase feasible and non-feasible index sets

\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\{t\in[T]:v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\},\qquad\mathcal{B}\coloneqq[T]\setminus\mathcal{A}.

F.1
Constrained SGM via Unified Estimation, Theorem
6.3
.

Suppose Assumption
6.2
hold. Suppose there exists a deterministic quantity

\mathcal{E}\geq 0
such that

\mathbb{P}\!\left(|e_{t}|\leq\mathcal{E}\quad\text{for all }t\in[T]\right)\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{2}.

(84)

Define

\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)\coloneqq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2DG}{\sqrt{T}}\sqrt{2\log\frac{4}{\delta}}.

Set the switching threshold to

\varepsilon\coloneqq\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)+\mathcal{E}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the selected set
\mathcal{A}
is non-empty,
and the active-phase selected average

\bar{w}\coloneqq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}w_{t}

satisfies

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon,\qquad h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon+\mathcal{E},

where
w^{\star}
is any optimal feasible solution.

In all family and parameter regimes considered below, the envelope
\mathcal{E}
in (
84
) is verified by specializing Theorem
4.2
and bounding the resulting estimator error uniformly over
t\in[T]
.

Proof.

We work on the intersection of the following two high-probability events:

•

the tail-envelope event from (
84
),

•

the martingale concentration event established below.

By a union bound, this intersection of the two high-probability events has probability at least
1-\delta
.

Step 1: Mirror-descent inequality on the active phase.

By the first-order optimality condition (see Remark
A.5
of First-Order Optimality Condition) for the mirror-descent update
such that
\langle\eta_{t}U_{t}+\nabla\Phi(w_{t+1})-\nabla\Phi(w_{t}),\,w^{\star}-w_{t+1}\rangle\geq 0
,
and using the three-point identity for Bregman divergences, we obtain

\eta_{t}\langle U_{t},w_{t+1}-w^{\star}\rangle\leq[D_{\Phi}(w^{\star},w_{t})-D_{\Phi}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})]-D_{\Phi}(w_{t+1},w_{t}).

By the
1
-strong convexity of
\Phi
, we obtain
D_{\Phi}(w_{t+1},w_{t})\geq\frac{1}{2}\|w_{t}-w_{t+1}\|^{2}=\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}}{2}\|P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})\|^{2}
,
also noting that
\|U_{t}\|_{\ast}^{2}+\|P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})\|^{2}\geq 2\|U_{t}\|_{\ast}\|P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})\|\geq 2\langle U_{t},P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})\rangle
, hence,

\displaystyle\langle U_{t},w_{t}-w^{\star}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle U_{t},w_{t+1}-w^{\star}\rangle+\langle U_{t},w_{t}-w_{t+1}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{t}}[D_{\Phi}(w^{\star},w_{t})-D_{\Phi}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})]-\frac{\eta_{t}}{2}\|P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})\|^{2}+\eta_{t}\langle U_{t},P(w_{t},U_{t},\eta_{t})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{t}}[D_{\Phi}(w^{\star},w_{t})-D_{\Phi}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})]+\frac{\eta_{t}}{2}\|U_{t}\|_{\ast}^{2}

Summing over
t\in[T]
and using
\eta_{t}=\eta
yields

\sum_{t\in[T]}\langle U_{t},w_{t}-w^{\star}\rangle\leq\frac{D_{\Phi}(w^{\star},w_{1})-D_{\Phi}(w^{\star},w_{T+1})}{\eta}+\frac{\eta}{2}\sum_{t\in[T]}\|U_{t}\|_{\ast}^{2}.

Since
\sup_{u,v\in\mathcal{W}}D_{\Phi}(u,v)\leq R^{2}/2
and
\|U_{t}\|_{\ast}\leq G
almost surely, and
D_{\Phi}(w^{\star},w_{T+1})\geq 0
,

\sum_{t\in[T]}\langle U_{t},w_{t}-w^{\star}\rangle\leq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta}+\frac{\eta TG^{2}}{2}.

(85)

Step 2: Convexity decomposition and martingale concentration.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{t}:=\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\xi_{t}^{B}\mid_{b_{t}=1},\xi_{t}\mid_{b_{t}=0},b_{t})
denote the sigma-field generated by the history
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
and the estimator randomness up to the switching decision (which also depends on
\xi_{t}^{B}\mid_{b_{t}=1},\xi_{t}\mid_{b_{t}=0},b_{t}
) at iteration
t
. By construction, the indicator
I_{t}=\mathbb{I}_{\{v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}}
is
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-measurable,

w_{t}-w^{\star}
is
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-measurable, while
\zeta_{t}
is independent of
\mathcal{G}_{t}
.
Define

g_{t}^{f}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[F^{{}^{\prime}}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})\mid\mathcal{G}_{t}]\in\partial f(w_{t}),\qquad g_{t}^{h}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[H^{{}^{\prime}}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})\mid\mathcal{G}_{t}]\in\partial h(w_{t}).

Also define

M_{t}\coloneqq I_{t}\langle F^{{}^{\prime}}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})-g_{t}^{f},\;w_{t}-w^{\star}\rangle+(1-I_{t})\langle H^{{}^{\prime}}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})-g_{t}^{h},\;w_{t}-w^{\star}\rangle.

By the conditional unbiasedness assumption,

\mathbb{E}[M_{t}\mid\mathcal{G}_{t}]=0,

so
\{M_{t}\}_{t\in[T]}
is a martingale difference sequence.
Moreover,

|M_{t}|\leq 2G\|w_{t}-w^{\star}\|\leq 2DG.

Hence, Azuma–Hoeffding inequality implies that with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

-\sum_{t\in[T]}M_{t}\leq 2DG\sqrt{2T\log\frac{4}{\delta}}.

By convexity of
f
and
h
, and
w^{\star}
is feasible,
h(w^{\star})\leq 0
, hence

\langle g_{t}^{f},w_{t}-w^{\star}\rangle\geq f(w_{t})-f(w^{\star}),\qquad\langle g_{t}^{h},w_{t}-w^{\star}\rangle\geq h(w_{t})-h(w^{\star})\geq h(w_{t}).

Therefore,

\sum_{t\in[T]}\langle U_{t},w_{t}-w^{\star}\rangle\geq\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(f(w_{t})-f(w^{\star})\bigr)+\sum_{t\in\mathcal{B}}h(w_{t})+\sum_{t\in[T]}M_{t}.

Combining this with (
85
), dividing by
T
, and using the Azuma bound gives

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(f(w_{t})-f(w^{\star})\bigr)+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{B}}h(w_{t})\leq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2DG}{\sqrt{T}}\sqrt{2\log\frac{4}{\delta}}=:\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta).

(86)

Now use
h(w_{t})=v_{t}-e_{t}\geq v_{t}-|e_{t}|
on
\mathcal{B}
:

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(f(w_{t})-f(w^{\star})\bigr)+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{B}}v_{t}\leq\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{B}}|e_{t}|.

On the tail-envelope event,
|e_{t}|\leq\mathcal{E}
for all
t\in[T]
, hence by letting
\varepsilon=\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)+\mathcal{E}
,

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(f(w_{t})-f(w^{\star})\bigr)+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{B}}v_{t}\leq\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)+\mathcal{E}=\varepsilon.

Thus

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(f(w_{t})-f(w^{\star})\bigr)+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{B}}v_{t}\leq\varepsilon.

(87)

Step 3: Non-emptiness of the selected set.

Assume for contradiction that
\mathcal{A}=\varnothing
. Then
\mathcal{B}=[T]
, and by definition of
\mathcal{B}
we have
v_{t}>\varepsilon
for every
t\in\mathcal{B}
. Therefore

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{B}}v_{t}>\varepsilon,

which contradicts (
87
). Hence
\mathcal{A}\neq\varnothing
.

Step 4: Optimality of the selected average.

If

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(f(w_{t})-f(w^{\star})\bigr)\leq 0,

then convexity of
f
implies

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq 0\leq\varepsilon.

Otherwise, the sum over
\mathcal{A}
is positive. Since
v_{t}>\varepsilon
for all
t\in\mathcal{B}
, (
87
) implies

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(f(w_{t})-f(w^{\star})\bigr)<\varepsilon-\frac{|\mathcal{B}|}{T}\varepsilon=\frac{|\mathcal{A}|}{T}\varepsilon.

Dividing by
|\mathcal{A}|/T
and using Jensen’s inequality yields

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(f(w_{t})-f(w^{\star})\bigr)<\varepsilon.

Hence

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon.

Step 5: Feasibility of the selected average.

By the convexity of
h
,

h(\bar{w})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}h(w_{t})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(v_{t}-e_{t}\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(v_{t}+|e_{t}|\bigr).

Since
v_{t}\leq\varepsilon
on
\mathcal{A}
and
|e_{t}|\leq\mathcal{E}
on the entire active phase,

h(\bar{w})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl(\varepsilon+\mathcal{E}\bigr)=\varepsilon+\mathcal{E}.

This completes the proof.
∎

F.2
Constrained SGM on a Reusable Analysis Window.

We can extend the above analysis to a selected set such that for
t_{0}<T
and
T_{a}:=T-t_{0}

\mathcal{A}:=\{t\in[T]:t>t_{0},v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}

By letting the threshold be

\varepsilon:=\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T_{a}}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2DG}{\sqrt{T_{a}}}\sqrt{2\log\frac{4}{\delta}}+\mathcal{E},

and
\mathbb{P}(\sup_{t\in[t_{0}+1:T]}|e_{t}|>\mathcal{E})\leq\delta/2
,
then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the selected set
\mathcal{A}
is non-empty, and the uniform average
\bar{w}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}w_{t}
satisfies both
f(\bar{w})-f\left(w^{*}\right)\leq\varepsilon
and
h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon+\mathcal{E}
.

F.3
Constrained Case 1 Bounds for Families 1–3.

1.

Choose step size

Family 3

\displaystyle+4DG\sqrt{\frac{\log\frac{4}{\delta}}{T}}

By letting

in Lemma

, selecting the step size and momentum parameter such that
\eta=\min\Big\{\left(\frac{C_{1}}{C_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{-\frac{1}{2}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{C_{1}^{2}}{C_{4}C_{5}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}T^{-\frac{2}{3}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{C_{1}^{3}}{C_{4}^{2}C_{6}}\right)^{\frac{1}{5}}T^{-\frac{3}{5}}\Big\}=\min\Big\{\frac{\sqrt{2}R}{G}T^{-\frac{1}{2}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{4}}{32\gamma G\sigma\Gamma_{T}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}T^{-\frac{2}{3}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{6}}{32\alpha G^{2}\sigma^{2}\Gamma_{T}}\right)^{\frac{1}{5}}T^{-\frac{3}{5}}\Big\}
,
the momentum parameter
\beta
as

1-\beta=\max\Big\{\left(\frac{C_{3}}{C_{4}}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}T^{-\frac{2}{3}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{C_{1}C_{5}}{C_{4}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}T^{-\frac{2}{3}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{C_{1}^{2}C_{6}}{C_{4}^{3}}\right)^{\frac{2}{5}}T^{-\frac{4}{5}}\Big\}=\max\Big\{2^{-\frac{1}{3}}T^{-\frac{2}{3}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{4}\gamma^{2}G^{2}/\sigma^{4}}{32\Gamma_{T}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}T^{-\frac{2}{3}},\allowbreak\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{R^{8}\alpha^{2}G^{4}/\sigma^{6}}{\Gamma_{T}^{3}}\right)^{\frac{1}{5}}T^{-\frac{4}{5}}\Big\}
,
then when
T\Gamma_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{R^{2}\gamma G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}

and
T\Gamma_{T}^{\frac{3}{4}}\geq 2^{-\frac{15}{4}}\left(\frac{R^{2}\sqrt{\alpha}G}{\sigma}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}
, we have:

\displaystyle\max\{f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\ast}),h(\bar{w})\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left(\frac{C_{1}C_{2}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+2\left(\frac{C_{3}C_{4}^{2}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+3\left(\frac{C_{1}C_{4}C_{5}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+3\left(\frac{C_{1}^{2}C_{4}^{2}C_{6}}{T^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{5}}+4DG\left(\frac{\log\frac{4}{\delta}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}RGT^{-\frac{1}{2}}+8\sigma\left(\frac{2\Gamma_{T}^{\frac{3}{2}}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+3(32R^{2}\gamma G\sigma)^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{T}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+6(R^{4}\alpha G^{2}\sigma^{2})^{\frac{1}{5}}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{T}}{T^{2}}\right)^{1/5}

\displaystyle+4DG\left(\frac{\log\frac{4}{\delta}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

∎

For later use in the complexity bounds, define

\mathcal{L}_{q}(u,\delta;c)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{e}^{q^{2}}}{u}\log^{q}\!\left(\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{c}{u\delta}\right),

q>0,\ u>0,\ c>0
.
By Lemma
D.4
,

T\geq\mathcal{L}_{q}(u,\delta;c)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{\log^{q}(cT/\delta)}{T}\leq u.

F.4
Constrained Case 1 Oracle Complexity for Families 1–3.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
F.3
, let
\varepsilon>0
, the iteration / oracle complexities to guarantee
f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon,h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon
for Families 1-3 are listed as follows, since
B=1
is selected as the batch size, the oracle complexity has the same order of the iteration complexity.

1.

Family 1.

N,\,T=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{R^{2}LG\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\log\frac{R^{2}LG\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\delta}\vee\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\log^{3/2}\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}\delta}\vee\frac{R^{2}G^{2}+D^{2}G^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Bigg).

2.

Family 2.

N,\,T=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\log^{3/2}\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}\delta}\vee\frac{R^{2}\ell G\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}}\log\frac{R^{2}\ell G\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}\delta}\vee\frac{R^{2}G^{2}+D^{2}G^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Bigg).

3.

Family 3.

\displaystyle N,\,T=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\log^{3/2}\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}\delta}\vee\frac{R^{2}\gamma G\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}}\log\frac{R^{2}\gamma G\sigma}{\varepsilon^{3}\delta}

\displaystyle\vee\sqrt{\frac{R^{4}\alpha G^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{5}}}\log^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\sqrt{\frac{R^{4}\alpha G^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{5}}}\right)

\displaystyle\vee\frac{R^{2}G^{2}+D^{2}G^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Bigg).

Proof.

Family 1
. To guarantee

\displaystyle\max\{f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star}),h(\bar{w})\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Q_{T}^{(1,1)}\equiv\left(\sqrt{2}RG+4DG\sqrt{\log\frac{4}{\delta}}\right)T^{-1/2}+8\sigma\left(\frac{\Gamma_{T}^{\frac{3}{2}}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+3\left(32R^{2}LG\sigma^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{T}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/4+\varepsilon/4=\varepsilon

with sufficient large
T
, where
\Gamma_{T}:=2\log\frac{4T}{\delta}
satisfies
T\Gamma_{T}\geq T\geq\frac{R^{2}LG}{8\sigma^{2}}
.
By noting
T\geq\mathcal{L}_{q}(u,\delta;c)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{q^{2}}}{u}\log^{q}(\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{c}{u\delta})
is sufficient to show
\frac{\log^{q}\frac{cT}{\delta}}{T}\leq u
(see Lemma
D.4
), we can choose
T
as follow to ensure
\max\{f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star}),h(\bar{w})\}\leq\varepsilon
.

\displaystyle T\geq

\displaystyle\frac{R^{2}LG}{8\sigma^{2}}\vee(\varepsilon/2)^{-2}\left(\sqrt{2}RG+4DG\sqrt{\log\frac{4}{\delta}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\vee\frac{(32\sqrt{2}\sigma)^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\mathrm{e}^{9/4}\log^{3/2}\left[\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{(32\sqrt{2}\sigma)^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot\frac{4}{\delta}\right]

\displaystyle\vee\frac{64R^{2}LG\sigma^{2}}{(\varepsilon/12)^{4}}\mathrm{e}\log\left[\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{64R^{2}LG\sigma^{2}}{(\varepsilon/12)^{4}}\cdot\frac{4}{\delta}\right]

Family 2
. To guarantee

\displaystyle\max\{f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star}),h(\bar{w})\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Q_{T}^{(2,1)}\equiv\left(\sqrt{2}RG+4DG\sqrt{\log\frac{4}{\delta}}\right)T^{-1/2}+8\sigma\left(\frac{2\Gamma_{T}^{\frac{3}{2}}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+3\left(32R^{2}\ell G\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{T}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/4+\varepsilon/4=\varepsilon

with sufficient large
T
, where
\Gamma_{T}:=2\log\frac{4T}{\delta}
satisfies
T\Gamma_{T}\geq T\geq\frac{R^{2}\ell G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}
.
By noting
T\geq\mathcal{L}_{q}(u,\delta;c)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{q^{2}}}{u}\log^{q}(\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{c}{u\delta})
is sufficient to show
\frac{\log^{q}\frac{cT}{\delta}}{T}\leq u
(see Lemma
D.4
), we can choose
T
as follow to ensure
\max\{f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star}),h(\bar{w})\}\leq\varepsilon
.

\displaystyle T\geq

\displaystyle\frac{R^{2}\ell G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}\vee(\varepsilon/2)^{-2}\left(\sqrt{2}RG+4DG\sqrt{\log\frac{4}{\delta}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\vee\frac{2(32\sqrt{2}\sigma)^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\vee\mathrm{e}^{9/4}\log^{3/2}\left[\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{2(32\sqrt{2}\sigma)^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot\frac{4}{\delta}\right]

\displaystyle\vee\frac{64R^{2}\ell G\sigma}{(\varepsilon/12)^{3}}\mathrm{e}\log\left[\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{64R^{2}\ell G\sigma}{(\varepsilon/12)^{3}}\cdot\frac{4}{\delta}\right]

Family 3
. To guarantee

\displaystyle\max\{f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star}),h(\bar{w})\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Q_{T}^{(3,1)}\equiv\left(\sqrt{2}RG+4DG\sqrt{\log\frac{4}{\delta}}\right)T^{-1/2}+8\sigma\left(\frac{2\Gamma_{T}^{\frac{3}{2}}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+3\left(32R^{2}\gamma G\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{T}}{T}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle+6(R^{4}\alpha G^{2}\sigma^{2})^{\frac{1}{5}}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{T}}{T^{2}}\right)^{1/5}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon/4+\varepsilon/4+\varepsilon/4+\varepsilon/4=\varepsilon

with sufficient large
T
, where
\Gamma_{T}:=2\log\frac{4T}{\delta}
satisfies
T\Gamma_{T}\geq T\geq\frac{R^{2}\gamma G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}
and
T\Gamma_{T}^{3/4}\geq 2^{-15/4}\left(\frac{R^{2}\sqrt{\alpha}G}{\sigma}\right)^{3/2}
.
By noting
T\geq\mathcal{L}_{q}(u,\delta;c)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{q^{2}}}{u}\log^{q}(\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{c}{u\delta})
is sufficient to show
\frac{\log^{q}\frac{cT}{\delta}}{T}\leq u
(see Lemma
D.4
), we can choose
T
as follow to ensure
\max\{f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star}),h(\bar{w})\}\leq\varepsilon
.

\displaystyle T\geq

\displaystyle\frac{R^{2}\gamma G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}\vee 2^{-15/4}\left(\frac{R^{2}\sqrt{\alpha}G}{\sigma}\right)^{3/2}\vee(\varepsilon/4)^{-2}\left(\sqrt{2}RG+4DG\sqrt{\log\frac{4}{\delta}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\vee\frac{2(32\sqrt{2}\sigma)^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\mathrm{e}^{9/4}\log^{3/2}\left[\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{2(32\sqrt{2}\sigma)^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot\frac{4}{\delta}\right]\vee\frac{64R^{2}\gamma G\sigma}{(\varepsilon/12)^{3}}\mathrm{e}\log\left[\mathrm{e}\vee\frac{64R^{2}\gamma G\sigma}{(\varepsilon/12)^{3}}\cdot\frac{4}{\delta}\right]

\displaystyle\vee\sqrt{\frac{2R^{4}\alpha G^{2}\sigma^{2}}{(\varepsilon/24)^{5}}}\mathrm{e}^{1/4}\log^{1/2}\left[\mathrm{e}\vee\sqrt{\frac{2R^{4}\alpha G^{2}\sigma^{2}}{(\varepsilon/24)^{5}}}\cdot\frac{4}{\delta}\right]

∎

F.2 Case 2 for Families 1–3 in the constrained setting

We now specialize Theorem
F.1
to the probabilistic-reset setting with

p=\frac{1}{B}\in(0,1]
. Since Application 2 estimates the scalar constraint value, we have

(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})=(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|)
, and hence
\kappa=1
. Let

\Lambda_{T}\coloneqq\log\frac{8T}{\delta},

C_{T}\coloneqq C\!\left(\frac{\delta}{8T},1\right),
and
\Gamma_{T}\coloneqq 2\Lambda_{T}.

Then, for
T\geq 1
, we have
\Lambda_{T}>1
, and

C_{T}=1+\sqrt{3\Lambda_{T}}\leq 2\sqrt{\Gamma_{T}}
.

F.5
Constrained Case 2 Bounds for Families 1–3.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
F.1
. For each family below, let

\mathcal{A}=\{t\in[T]:v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}
,

\varepsilon=\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)+\mathcal{E}
, and

\bar{w}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}w_{t}
.

1.

Family 1.

Assume that, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

|e_{t}|\leq C_{T}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{B}}+\frac{GL\eta}{p}\Lambda_{T},\qquad\forall t\in[T].

Choose

\eta=\min\Big\{\frac{R}{G\sqrt{T}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{6}}{256GL\sigma^{2}\Lambda_{T}^{2}T^{3}}\right)^{1/4}\Big\},

B=\left\lceil\left(\frac{4\sigma^{2}T}{R^{2}GL}\right)^{1/2}\right\rceil,

p=\frac{1}{B}.

Assume moreover that

T\geq\frac{R^{2}GL}{4\sigma^{2}}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq Q_{T}^{(1,2)},

h(\bar{w})\leq Q_{T}^{(1,2)},

where

Q_{T}^{(1,2)}=\frac{RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+4\left(\frac{16R^{2}GL\sigma^{2}\Lambda_{T}^{2}}{T}\right)^{1/4}.

2.

Family 2.

Assume that, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

|e_{t}|\leq C_{T}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{B}}+C_{T}\ell\eta G\sqrt{\frac{2\Lambda_{T}}{p}},\qquad\forall t\in[T].

Choose

\eta=\min\Big\{\frac{R}{G\sqrt{T}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{4}}{256\sigma\ell G\Lambda_{T}^{3/2}T^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\Big\},

B=\left\lceil\left(\frac{32\sigma^{4}T^{2}}{R^{4}\ell^{2}G^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\right\rceil,

p=\frac{1}{B}.

Assume moreover that

T\geq\frac{R^{2}\ell G}{4\sqrt{2}\,\sigma^{2}}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq Q_{T}^{(2,2)},

h(\bar{w})\leq Q_{T}^{(2,2)},

where

Q_{T}^{(2,2)}=\frac{RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+3\left(\frac{32R^{2}\sigma\ell G\Lambda_{T}^{3/2}}{T}\right)^{1/3}.

3.

Family 3.

Assume that, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

|e_{t}|\leq C_{T}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{B}}+\frac{\alpha\eta^{2}G^{2}}{2p}\Lambda_{T}+C_{T}\gamma\eta G\sqrt{\frac{2\Lambda_{T}}{p}},\qquad\forall t\in[T].

Choose

\eta=\min\Big\{\frac{R}{G\sqrt{T}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{4}}{256\sigma\gamma G\Lambda_{T}^{3/2}T^{2}}\right)^{1/3},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{6}}{1024\alpha G^{2}\sigma^{2}\Lambda_{T}^{2}T^{3}}\right)^{1/5}\Big\},

B=\left\lceil\min\Big\{\left(\frac{32\sigma^{4}T^{2}}{R^{4}\gamma^{2}G^{2}}\right)^{1/3},\allowbreak\left(\frac{32768\sigma^{6}\Lambda_{T}T^{4}}{\alpha^{2}R^{8}G^{4}}\right)^{1/5}\Big\}\right\rceil,

p=\frac{1}{B}.

Assume moreover that

T\geq\frac{R^{2}\gamma G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}},

2^{3/2}T^{2}\Lambda_{T}^{1/2}\geq\frac{\alpha R^{4}G^{2}}{64\sigma^{3}}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq Q_{T}^{(3,2)},

h(\bar{w})\leq Q_{T}^{(3,2)},

where

Q_{T}^{(3,2)}=\frac{RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+3\left(\frac{32R^{2}\sigma\gamma G\Lambda_{T}^{3/2}}{T}\right)^{\tfrac{1}{3}}+5\left(\frac{\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}\Lambda_{T}^{2}}{T^{2}}\right)^{\tfrac{1}{5}}.

Proof.

Since each estimator bound holds uniformly for all
t\in[T]
(substituting the high-probability upperbound
\mathcal{E}
of
|e_{t}|
from the instantiations of Theorem
4.2
, replacing
\delta
with
\delta/2
and selecting
B=1/p
in the bound of Table
2
), (
84
) in Theorem
F.1
holds. Hence, with

\varepsilon=\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)+\mathcal{E},

Theorem
F.1
yields

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq Q,\qquad h(\bar{w})\leq Q,\qquad Q\coloneqq\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)+2\mathcal{E}.

In every family,

\frac{2DG}{\sqrt{T}}\sqrt{2\log\frac{4}{\delta}}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}.

Also,

2C_{T}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{B}}\leq 4\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma_{T}}{B}}=4\sigma\sqrt{p\Gamma_{T}}=4\sigma\sqrt{2p\Lambda_{T}}.

For
Family 1
,

Q\leq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+4\sigma\sqrt{2p\Lambda_{T}}+\frac{2GL\Lambda_{T}}{p}\eta.

Set

C_{1}=\frac{R^{2}}{2},C_{2}=\frac{G^{2}}{2},C_{4}=4\sigma\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}},C_{5}=2GL\Lambda_{T}.

Then

Q\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{p}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}.

Lemma
D.3
with
C_{3}=0
gives the stated choice of
(\eta,B,p)
and yields

\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{p}\leq\frac{RG}{\sqrt{T}}+4\left(\frac{16R^{2}GL\sigma^{2}\Lambda_{T}^{2}}{T}\right)^{1/4}.

For
Family 2
, using
C_{T}\leq 2\sqrt{\Gamma_{T}}=2\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}}
, we get

Q\leq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+4\sigma\sqrt{2p\Lambda_{T}}+8\ell G\Lambda_{T}\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{p}}.

Set

C_{1}=\frac{R^{2}}{2},C_{2}=\frac{G^{2}}{2},C_{4}=4\sigma\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}},C_{5}=8\ell G\Lambda_{T}.

Then

Q\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{\sqrt{p}}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}.

Lemma
D.3
yields

\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{\sqrt{p}}\leq\frac{RG}{\sqrt{T}}+3\left(\frac{32R^{2}\sigma\ell G\Lambda_{T}^{3/2}}{T}\right)^{1/3}.

For
Family 3
, the same estimate gives

Q\leq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+4\sigma\sqrt{2p\Lambda_{T}}+8\gamma G\Lambda_{T}\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{p}}+\alpha G^{2}\Lambda_{T}\frac{\eta^{2}}{p}.

Set

C_{1}=\frac{R^{2}}{2},\quad C_{2}=\frac{G^{2}}{2},\quad C_{4}=4\sigma\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}},\quad C_{5}=8\gamma G\Lambda_{T},\quad C_{6}=\alpha G^{2}\Lambda_{T}.

Then

Q\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{\sqrt{p}}+\frac{C_{6}\eta^{2}}{p}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}.

Lemma
D.3
yields

\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{\sqrt{p}}+\frac{C_{6}\eta^{2}}{p}\leq\frac{RG}{\sqrt{T}}+3\left(\frac{32R^{2}\sigma\gamma G\Lambda_{T}^{3/2}}{T}\right)^{1/3}+5\left(\frac{\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}\Lambda_{T}^{2}}{T^{2}}\right)^{1/5}.

Combining these gives the three stated bounds.
∎

F.6
Constrained Case 2 Iteration Complexity for Families 1–3.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
F.5
, let
\varepsilon>0
.

1.

Family 1.

Define

A_{1}\coloneqq RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)},

A_{2}\coloneqq 4(16R^{2}GL\sigma^{2})^{1/4},

u_{2}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2A_{2}}\right)^{4}.

If

T\geq\frac{4A_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\vee\mathcal{L}_{2}\!\left(u_{2},\delta;8\right)\vee\left\lceil\frac{R^{2}GL}{4\sigma^{2}}\right\rceil,

then

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon,

h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon.

Moreover,

T=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R^{2}GL\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\log^{2}\frac{R^{2}GL\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\delta}\vee\frac{R^{2}G^{2}+D^{2}G^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

2.

Family 2.

Define

A_{1}\coloneqq RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)},

A_{2}\coloneqq 3(32R^{2}\sigma\ell G)^{1/3},

u_{2}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2A_{2}}\right)^{3}.

If

T\geq\frac{4A_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\vee\mathcal{L}_{\frac{3}{2}}\!\left(u_{2},\delta;8\right)\vee\left\lceil\frac{R^{2}\ell G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}\right\rceil,

then

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon,

h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon.

Moreover,

T=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R^{2}\sigma\ell G}{\varepsilon^{3}}\log^{3/2}\frac{R^{2}\sigma\ell G}{\varepsilon^{3}\delta}\vee\frac{R^{2}G^{2}+D^{2}G^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

3.

Family 3.

Define

A_{1}\coloneqq RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)},

A_{2}\coloneqq 3(32R^{2}\sigma\gamma G)^{1/3},A_{3}\coloneqq 5\left(\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}\right)^{1/5},

u_{2}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{3A_{2}}\right)^{3},

u_{3}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{3A_{3}}\right)^{5}.

If

T\geq\frac{9A_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\vee\mathcal{L}_{\frac{3}{2}}\!\left(u_{2},\delta;8\right)\vee\mathcal{L}_{1}\!\left(\sqrt{u_{3}},\delta;8\right)\vee\left\lceil\frac{R^{2}\gamma G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}\right\rceil\vee\left\lceil\left(\frac{\alpha R^{4}G^{2}}{64\sigma^{3}}\right)^{1/2}\right\rceil,

then

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon,

h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon.

Moreover,

\displaystyle T=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{R^{2}\sigma\gamma G}{\varepsilon^{3}}\log^{3/2}\frac{R^{2}\sigma\gamma G}{\varepsilon^{3}\delta}

\displaystyle\vee\sqrt{\frac{\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}}{\varepsilon^{5}}}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}}{\varepsilon^{5}}}\right)

\displaystyle\vee\frac{R^{2}G^{2}+D^{2}G^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Bigg).

In all three families, the expected oracle complexity is

N=[B\cdot p+1\cdot(1-p)]T=(2-1/B)T<2T,

hence is of the same order as
T
.

Proof.

As in the previous corollary, it is enough to make every term of the corresponding
Q
-bound at most
\varepsilon/r
, where
r=2
for Families 1 and 2 and
r=3
for Family 3. Since

\Lambda_{T}=\log(8T/\delta)
, we use

\frac{\Lambda_{T}^{2}}{T}\leq u\Leftarrow T\geq\mathcal{L}_{2}\!\left(u,\delta;8\right),

\frac{\Lambda_{T}^{3/2}}{T}\leq u\Leftarrow T\geq\mathcal{L}_{\frac{3}{2}}\!\left(u,\delta;8\right),

and

\frac{\Lambda_{T}^{2}}{T^{2}}\leq u\Leftarrow T\geq\mathcal{L}_{1}\!\left(\sqrt{u},\delta;8\right).

Applying these implications to the three
Q
-bounds, together with the side conditions in the theorem, gives the stated lower bounds on
T
. The displayed asymptotic orders follow by collecting the largest
\varepsilon
-dependences. Finally,

N=(2-1/B)T<2T.

∎

F.3 Case 3 for Families 1–3 in the constrained setting

In Case 3, the reset period is deterministic. We write

E\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

p_{t}=\mathbb{I}_{\{t\bmod E=0\}},

B=E.

Since Application 2 estimates the scalar constraint value, we have

(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})=(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|)
, and hence
\kappa=1
. Let

\Lambda_{T}\coloneqq\log\frac{8T}{\delta},

C_{T}\coloneqq C\!\left(\frac{\delta}{8T},1\right),
and
\Gamma_{T}\coloneqq 2\Lambda_{T}.

Then, for
T\geq 1
, we have
\Lambda_{T}>1
, and

C_{T}=1+\sqrt{3\Lambda_{T}}\leq 2\sqrt{\Gamma_{T}}
.

F.7
Constrained Case 3 Tuned Bounds for Families 1–3.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
F.1
. For each family below, let

\varepsilon\coloneqq\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)+\mathcal{E}
,

\mathcal{A}=\{t\in[T]:v_{t}\leq\varepsilon\}
, and

\bar{w}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}}w_{t}
.

1.

Family 1.

Assume that, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

|e_{t}|\leq C_{T}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{E}}+GL\eta E,\qquad\forall t\in[T].

Choose

\eta=\min\Big\{\frac{R}{G\sqrt{T}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{6}}{256GL\sigma^{2}\Lambda_{T}T^{3}}\right)^{1/4}\Big\},

E=B=\left\lceil\left(\frac{4\sigma^{2}\Lambda_{T}T}{R^{2}GL}\right)^{1/2}\right\rceil.

Assume moreover that

T\Lambda_{T}\geq\frac{R^{2}GL}{4\sigma^{2}}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq Q_{T}^{(1,3)},

h(\bar{w})\leq Q_{T}^{(1,3)},

where

Q_{T}^{(1,3)}=\frac{RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+4\left(\frac{16R^{2}GL\sigma^{2}\Lambda_{T}}{T}\right)^{1/4}.

2.

Family 2.

Assume that, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

|e_{t}|\leq C_{T}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{E}}+C_{T}\ell\eta G\sqrt{2E},\qquad\forall t\in[T].

Choose

\eta=\min\Big\{\frac{R}{G\sqrt{T}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{4}}{256\sigma\ell G\Lambda_{T}T^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\Big\},

E=B=\left\lceil\left(\frac{32\sigma^{4}\Lambda_{T}T^{2}}{R^{4}\ell^{2}G^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\right\rceil.

Assume moreover that

T\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}}\geq\frac{R^{2}\ell G}{4\sigma^{2}}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq Q_{T}^{(2,3)},

h(\bar{w})\leq Q_{T}^{(2,3)},

where

Q_{T}^{(2,3)}=\frac{RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+3\left(\frac{32R^{2}\sigma\ell G\Lambda_{T}}{T}\right)^{1/3}.

3.

Family 3.

Assume that, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

|e_{t}|\leq C_{T}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{E}}+\frac{\alpha\eta^{2}G^{2}E}{2}+C_{T}\gamma\eta G\sqrt{2E},\qquad\forall t\in[T].

Choose

\eta=\min\Big\{\frac{R}{G\sqrt{T}},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{4}}{256\sigma\gamma G\Lambda_{T}T^{2}}\right)^{1/3},\allowbreak\left(\frac{R^{6}}{1024\alpha G^{2}\sigma^{2}\Lambda_{T}T^{3}}\right)^{1/5}\Big\},

E=B=\left\lceil\min\Big\{\left(\frac{32\sigma^{4}\Lambda_{T}T^{2}}{R^{4}\gamma^{2}G^{2}}\right)^{1/3},\allowbreak\left(\frac{32768\sigma^{6}\Lambda_{T}^{3}T^{4}}{\alpha^{2}R^{8}G^{4}}\right)^{1/5}\Big\}\right\rceil.

Assume moreover that

T\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}}\geq\frac{R^{2}\gamma G}{4\sigma^{2}},

2^{3/2}T^{2}\Lambda_{T}^{3/2}\geq\frac{\alpha R^{4}G^{2}}{64\sigma^{3}}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq Q_{T}^{(3,3)},

h(\bar{w})\leq Q_{T}^{(3,3)},

where

Q_{T}^{(3,3)}=\frac{RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+3\left(\frac{32R^{2}\sigma\gamma G\Lambda_{T}}{T}\right)^{1/3}+5\left(\frac{\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}\Lambda_{T}}{T^{2}}\right)^{1/5}.

Proof.

As in Case 2, the uniform estimator bound (substituting the high-probability upperbound
\mathcal{E}
of
|e_{t}|
from the instantiations of Theorem
4.2
, replacing
\delta
with
\delta/2
and selecting
B=E
in the bound of Table
2
) implies that Theorem
F.1
applies and

Q\coloneqq\mathsf{OptError}(\eta,T,\delta)+2\mathcal{E}.

Since
C_{T}\leq 2\sqrt{\Gamma_{T}}
,

2C_{T}\sigma/\sqrt{E}\leq 4\sigma\sqrt{\Gamma_{T}}/\sqrt{E}=4\sigma\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}}/\sqrt{E}.

For
Family 1
,

Q\leq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+4\sigma\frac{\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}}}{\sqrt{E}}+2GL\eta E.

Set

C_{1}=\frac{R^{2}}{2},

C_{2}=\frac{G^{2}}{2},

C_{4}=4\sigma\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}},

C_{5}=2GL.

Introducing
p=1/E
, this becomes

Q\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{p}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}.

Lemma
D.3
yields

\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{p}\leq\frac{RG}{\sqrt{T}}+4\left(\frac{16R^{2}GL\sigma^{2}\Lambda_{T}}{T}\right)^{1/4}.

For
Family 2
, using
C_{T}\leq 2\sqrt{\Gamma_{T}}=2\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}}
, we get

Q\leq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+4\sigma\frac{\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}}}{\sqrt{E}}+8\ell G\sqrt{\Lambda_{T}}\,\eta\sqrt{E}.

Set

C_{1}=\frac{R^{2}}{2},

C_{2}=\frac{G^{2}}{2},

C_{4}=4\sigma\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}},

C_{5}=8\ell G\sqrt{\Lambda_{T}}.

After setting
p=1/E
,

Q\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{\sqrt{p}}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}.

Lemma
D.3
yields

\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{\sqrt{p}}\leq\frac{RG}{\sqrt{T}}+3\left(\frac{32R^{2}\sigma\ell G\Lambda_{T}}{T}\right)^{1/3}.

For
Family 3
, the same estimate gives

Q\leq\frac{R^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta G^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}+4\sigma\frac{\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}}}{\sqrt{E}}+8\gamma G\sqrt{\Lambda_{T}}\,\eta\sqrt{E}+\alpha G^{2}\eta^{2}E.

Set

C_{1}=\frac{R^{2}}{2},

C_{2}=\frac{G^{2}}{2},

C_{4}=4\sigma\sqrt{2\Lambda_{T}},

C_{5}=8\gamma G\sqrt{\Lambda_{T}},

C_{6}=\alpha G^{2}.

After setting
p=1/E
,

Q\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{\sqrt{p}}+\frac{C_{6}\eta^{2}}{p}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)}}{\sqrt{T}}.

Lemma
D.3
yields

\frac{C_{1}}{\eta T}+C_{2}\eta+C_{4}\sqrt{p}+\frac{C_{5}\eta}{\sqrt{p}}+\frac{C_{6}\eta^{2}}{p}\leq\frac{RG}{\sqrt{T}}+3\left(\frac{32R^{2}\sigma\gamma G\Lambda_{T}}{T}\right)^{1/3}+5\left(\frac{\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}\Lambda_{T}}{T^{2}}\right)^{1/5}.

Combining these gives the bounds.
∎

F.8
Constrained Case 3 Iteration Complexity for Families 1–3.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
F.7
, let
\varepsilon>0
.

1.

Family 1.

Define

A_{1}\coloneqq RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)},

A_{2}\coloneqq 4(16R^{2}GL\sigma^{2})^{1/4},

u_{2}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2A_{2}}\right)^{4}.

If

T\geq\frac{4A_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\vee\mathcal{L}_{1}\left(u_{2},\delta;8\right)\vee\left\lceil\frac{R^{2}GL}{4\sigma^{2}}\right\rceil,

then

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon,

h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon.

Moreover,

T=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R^{2}GL\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\log\frac{R^{2}GL\sigma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\delta}\vee\frac{R^{2}G^{2}+D^{2}G^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

2.

Family 2.

Define

A_{1}\coloneqq RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)},

A_{2}\coloneqq 3(32R^{2}\sigma\ell G)^{1/3},

u_{2}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2A_{2}}\right)^{3}.

If

T\geq\frac{4A_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\vee\mathcal{L}_{1}\left(u_{2},\delta;8\right)\vee\left\lceil\frac{R^{2}\ell G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}\right\rceil,

then

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon,

h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon.

Moreover,

T=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{R^{2}\sigma\ell G}{\varepsilon^{3}}\log\frac{R^{2}\sigma\ell G}{\varepsilon^{3}\delta}\vee\frac{R^{2}G^{2}+D^{2}G^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

3.

Family 3.

Define

A_{1}\coloneqq RG+2\sqrt{2}\,DG\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)},

A_{2}\coloneqq 3(32R^{2}\sigma\gamma G)^{1/3},

A_{3}\coloneqq 5\left(\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}\right)^{1/5},

u_{2}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{3A_{2}}\right)^{3},

u_{3}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{3A_{3}}\right)^{5}.

If

T\geq\frac{9A_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\vee\mathcal{L}_{1}\left(u_{2},\delta;8\right)\vee\mathcal{L}_{1/2}\left(\sqrt{u_{3}},\delta;8\right)\vee\left\lceil\frac{R^{2}\gamma G}{4\sqrt{2}\sigma^{2}}\right\rceil\vee\left\lceil 2^{-3/4}\left(\frac{\alpha R^{4}G^{2}}{64\sigma^{3}}\right)^{1/2}\right\rceil,

then

f(\bar{w})-f(w^{\star})\leq\varepsilon,

h(\bar{w})\leq\varepsilon.

Moreover,

\displaystyle T=\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\frac{R^{2}\sigma\gamma G}{\varepsilon^{3}}\log\frac{R^{2}\sigma\gamma G}{\varepsilon^{3}\delta}

\displaystyle\vee\sqrt{\frac{\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}}{\varepsilon^{5}}}\log^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha R^{4}\sigma^{2}G^{2}}{\varepsilon^{5}}}\right)

\displaystyle\vee\frac{R^{2}G^{2}+D^{2}G^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Bigg).

In all three families, the deterministic oracle complexity satisfies

N=B\Bigl\lfloor\frac{T}{E}\Bigr\rfloor+\Bigl(T-\Bigl\lfloor\frac{T}{E}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)=T+(E-1)\Bigl\lfloor\frac{T}{E}\Bigr\rfloor<2T.

Proof.

As before, it is enough to make each term in the relevant bound
Q_{T}^{(i,3)}
at most
\varepsilon/r
, with
r=2
for Families 1 and 2 and
r=3
for Family 3. Since

\Lambda_{T}=\log(8T/\delta),

we use

\frac{\Lambda_{T}}{T}\leq u\Leftarrow T\geq\mathcal{L}_{1}\left(u,\delta;8\right),

and

\frac{\Lambda_{T}}{T^{2}}\leq u\Leftarrow T\geq\mathcal{L}_{1/2}\left(\sqrt{u},\delta;8\right).

Applying these implications to
Q_{T}^{(1,3)},Q_{T}^{(2,3)},Q_{T}^{(3,3)}
, together with the side conditions in the theorem, gives the stated lower bounds on
T
. The asymptotic statements follow by collecting the dominant
\varepsilon
-terms. The oracle bound is the displayed identity together with
\lfloor T/E\rfloor\leq T/E
.
∎

Appendix G
Related Work

Variance-reduced stochastic estimation.

The mathematical foundations of stochastic first-order methods have been extensively developed since the introduction of stochastic approximation, traditionally relying on uniformly bounded stochastic subgradients or globally bounded variance assumptions to establish convergence
[
118
,
16
,
48
,
114
,
103
,
9
,
115
,
123
,
19
,
75
,
121
,
55
]
. In smooth nonconvex optimization, vanilla SGD attains an
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-4})
oracle complexity for finding an
\varepsilon
-stationary point
[
47
]
. Variance reduction improves this benchmark through two broad routes: finite-sum control-variate methods such as SAG, SAGA, and SVRG
[
119
,
77
,
36
,
18
]
, which use periodic reference-gradient computation, and recursive estimators such as SARAH, SPIDER, STORM, and PAGE
[
108
,
43
,
33
,
133
,
88
,
86
,
27
,
34
,
62
]
, which update an online estimator via stochastic differences. Under mean-squared Lipschitz gradients, recursive estimators achieve the optimal
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-3})
complexity, matching lower-bound theory
[
7
,
39
]
. Second-order variants further improve the dependence on problem constants using Hessian-vector products while preserving the
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-3})
rate
[
6
]
, and higher-order methods under Lipschitz Hessians can reach rates of
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-3.5})
or better through cubic regularization or perturbed acceleration
[
142
,
4
,
134
,
44
]
. More recent works have also pursued broader viewpoints, including filtering-based perspectives
[
143
]
, manifold extensions such as SERENA
[
90
]
, and unified adaptive theories that relax unbiasedness
[
124
]
. On the practical side, MARS shows that recursive variance reduction can substantially accelerate large-model training when combined with preconditioned updates
[
144
]
. Our framework differs from these lines by making the
correction term
explicit, thereby isolating the mechanism that aligns stale information from
w_{t-1}
with the current iterate
w_{t}
and yielding a unified analysis that also suggests new second-order variants.

High-probability guarantees.

Most guarantees in stochastic optimization are stated only in expectation, which can mask rare but consequential failures. High-probability results are therefore especially important in nonconvex regimes where noise can substantially degrade the signal-to-noise ratio. For non-variance-reduced methods, such guarantees are available for convex SGD
[
59
]
, normalized SGD with momentum achieving
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-4})
complexity
[
31
]
, and heavy-tailed stochastic optimization in arbitrary smooth norms
[
32
]
. For recursive variance-reduced methods, however, high-probability analyses remain scarce and largely estimator-specific: existing results for PAGE
[
86
]
and STORM-type methods
[
141
]
do not directly transfer across estimator families. A unified high-probability treatment for multiple recursive estimators has been missing. Related efforts under relaxed noise assumptions include bounds under bounded
\alpha
-th central moments
[
122
]
and various heavy-tailed analyses
[
49
,
51
,
109
,
54
]
. These works address the noise-model axis, whereas our focus is on the estimator-design axis. Our main technical tool is a novel vector-valued Freedman inequality for
\kappa
-smooth Banach spaces, building on
[
112
,
78
]
; crucially, it accommodates the random predictable variance proxies and stopping-time intervals induced by algorithmic reset events, enabling a single, unified high-probability guarantee across all estimator families.

Relaxed variance assumptions.

To move beyond globally bounded variance, recent work has introduced structural relaxations such as expected smoothness and ABC-type conditions
[
50
,
80
,
81
,
68
,
52
]
, which control the variance by a constant plus terms depending on the gradient norm and suboptimality. The BG-0 condition is weaker still and captures realistic optimization trajectories without uniform noise bounds
[
2
,
137
,
30
,
8
,
71
,
129
,
38
,
107
]
. Recent lower bounds show that finding an
\varepsilon
-stationary point under BG-0 requires at least
\Omega(\varepsilon^{-6})
and
\Omega(\varepsilon^{-4})
queries in smooth and mean-square smooth settings, respectively
[
45
]
. Our sub-Gaussian oracle model is stronger than these relaxations, and extending the present unified high-probability framework to the ABC or BG-0 regime remains an important open problem.

Mirror descent and non-Euclidean optimization.

Mirror descent
[
105
,
14
]
generalizes projected gradient descent to non-Euclidean geometries through Bregman divergences and is particularly natural on domains such as the probability simplex. High-probability convergence for stochastic mirror descent without variance reduction has been established in convex settings under sub-Gaussian noise
[
92
]
and extended to heavy-tailed settings via clipping
[
109
]
. In nonconvex problems,
[
32
]
obtain high-probability guarantees for normalized SGD with momentum in smooth norms. However, these analyses do not incorporate recursive variance-reduced estimators within the mirror-descent framework. By formulating our estimator-level analysis directly in a
\kappa
-smooth normed space, we decouple the concentration argument from the specific update rule and obtain guarantees that apply when the estimator lives in the dual space, and iterates are updated by mirror descent. The parameter
\kappa
captures the geometric regularity, including
\kappa=1
in Hilbert spaces,
\kappa=q-1
in
\ell_{q}
for
q\geq 2
, and
\kappa=\mathcal{O}(\log d)
for smooth approximations of
\ell_{\infty}
(see Appendix
A
for detailed discussion).

Constrained stochastic optimization.

Stochastic optimization with expectation constraints (

‡

‣
1
) requires estimating both objective and feasibility quantities from noisy observations. Standard approaches include primal-dual, saddle-point, and augmented-Lagrangian methods
[
104
,
24
,
15
,
56
,
145
,
64
,
17
]
, which can be sensitive to dual tuning and bounded-domain projections. A primal-only alternative is the switching gradient method (SGM), originating in
[
113
]
, which alternates between objective decrease and constraint reduction. Its mirror-descent variants address non-smoothness and non-Euclidean geometry
[
131
,
127
,
3
,
125
,
126
,
128
,
130
,
13
]
. In the stochastic setting,
[
83
]
establish an
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-4})
oracle complexity for convex expectation-constrained problems using batch averaging for constraint estimation, and related weakly convex analyses retain the same order
[
65
,
89
,
98
,
76
,
35
,
40
,
99
]
. For nonconvex problems,
[
87
]
combine momentum-based variance reduction with an inexact augmented-Lagrangian framework and obtain an
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-5})
complexity in expectation; however, the method is a double-loop scheme, using PStorm as an inner subroutine. Our contribution is to show that replacing batch averaging in the SGM-style setting of
[
83
]
with recursive variance-reduced estimators from our unified framework yields improved high-probability oracle complexity bounds of order
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-3})
in the settings studied here.

Complementary variance reduction and gradient estimation strategies.

Beyond recursive estimators, variance can also be reduced through negative dependence. Antithetic variates are a classical example
[
58
,
79
,
120
,
57
,
140
]
. More recent work strengthens these guarantees through strongly isotonic assumptions and optimal-transport connections
[
61
,
60
]
(cf.
[
5
,
110
,
20
]
). The RAMPAGE framework
[
63
]
extends this viewpoint to variational inequalities through geometric path integration that removes first-order bias. Separately, exploiting structural properties for estimation, such as low rank, data smoothness, and interpolation, leads to improved rates in canonical settings
[
85
,
74
,
29
,
72
,
28
]
. These directions are complementary to our estimator design and suggest promising ways to enrich the correction term
\mathcal{T}_{t}
in future work.

Decentralized and federated optimization.

Distributed and federated optimization must balance local computation, communication, and statistical heterogeneity
[
100
,
117
,
111
,
138
,
10
,
139
,
25
,
73
]
. Gradient-tracking approaches such as Global Update Tracking (GUT) mitigate local drift without additional communication
[
1
]
, while switching-based and partially participating schemes address failures caused by heterogeneity
[
69
,
135
,
70
,
26
,
53
,
67
,
116
,
66
,
136
,
97
]
. Incorporating switching-based mechanics with partial client
participation mitigates systemic failures induced by statistical heterogeneity, appropriately shifting
the algorithmic focus from maximum likelihood estimation
[
94
,
95
,
96
]
to worst-case distributionally robust optimization.
Extending this robust methodology to fully adversarial environments, online learning in MDPs has likewise benefited from optimistic estimators with sublinear regret
[
102
]
. Since client drift compounds the temporal iterate movement that drives estimation error in our framework, extending the correction-term viewpoint to communication-constrained and decentralized settings may yield sharper communication-complexity guarantees.

Future directions.

Several directions remain open. Extending the unified high-probability theory to relaxed noise models such as BG-0
[
2
,
45
]
, and combining it with adaptive or hyperparameter-free step-size rules
[
124
,
41
,
82
,
37
,
93
,
101
,
23
]
, are especially natural next steps. Another promising direction is to transport the estimator-level perspective developed here to decentralized and federated settings, where client drift and communication delays create additional sources of tracking error.
